                                           A (2+ε)-Approximation Algorithm for Metric k-Median

                                                     Vincent Cohen-Addad*                      Fabrizio Grandoni†                  Euiwoong Lee‡
                                                                      Chris Schwiegelshohn §                       Ola Svensson¶


                                                                                                  Abstract
arXiv:2503.10972v2 [cs.DS] 19 May 2026




                                               In the classical NP-hard (metric) k-median problem, we are given a set of n clients and centers with
                                               metric distances between them, along with an integer parameter k ≥ 1. The objective is to select a
                                               subset of k open centers that minimizes the total distance from each client to its closest open center.
                                                   In their seminal work, Jain, Mahdian, Markakis, Saberi, and Vazirani presented the Greedy al-
                                               gorithm for facility location, which implies a 2-approximation algorithm for k-median that opens
                                               k centers in expectation. Since then, substantial research has aimed at narrowing the gap between
                                               their algorithm and the best achievable approximation by an algorithm guaranteed to open exactly
                                               k centers, as required in the k-median problem. During the last decade, all improvements have
                                               been achieved by leveraging their algorithm (or a small improvement thereof), followed by a sec-
                                               ond step called bi-point rounding, which inherently adds an additional factor to the approximation
                                               guarantee.
                                                   Our main result closes this gap: for any ε > 0, we present a (2 + ε)-approximation algorithm
                                               for the k-median problem, improving the previous best-known approximation factor of 2.613. Our
                                               approach builds on a combination of two key algorithms. First, we present a non-trivial modifi-
                                               cation of the Greedy algorithm that operates with only O(log n/ε2 ) adaptive phases. Through a
                                               novel walk-between-solutions approach, this enables us to construct a (2 + ε)-approximation algo-
                                               rithm for k-median that consistently opens at most k + O(log n/ε2 ) centers: via known results, this
                                               already implies a (2 + ε)-approximation algorithm that runs in quasi-polynomial time. Second, we
                                               develop a novel (2 + ε)-approximation algorithm tailored for stable instances, where removing any
                                               center from an optimal solution increases the cost by at least an Ω(ε3 / log n) fraction. Achieving this
                                               involves several ideas, including a sampling approach inspired by the k-means++ algorithm and a
                                               reduction to submodular optimization subject to a partition matroid. This allows us to convert the
                                               previous result into a polynomial time algorithm that opens exactly k centers while maintaining the
                                               (2 + ε)-approximation guarantee.

1       Introduction
Given a collection D of n points, a clustering is a partition of the points into (typically) disjoint subsets
(the clusters) such that points in the same cluster are similar and points in different clusters are dissim-
ilar. One of the classical and best-studied clustering problems is k-median (see also Table 1). Here, in
addition to the set D (points are also called clients in this framework), we are given an integer param-
eter k > 0 and a set F1 (the facilities or centers), with metric distances d( a, b) for a, b ∈ D ∪ F. Our goal
is to select k centers S ⊆ F (open centers) so as to minimize the sum of the distances from each client
to the closest open center. In other words, the goal is to minimize the following objective function

                                             cost(S) := ∑ min d(i, j).
                                                          j∈ D i ∈S

The k-clustering induced by S is obtained by considering, for each c ∈ S, the subset of points that
have c as a closest center in S (breaking ties arbitrarily). We use optk to denote the optimal cost, and
simply use opt when k is clear from the context.
    For most clustering variants, it is NP-hard to compute an optimal clustering and metric k-median
is no exception. For this reason a lot of effort was devoted to the design of (polynomial-time) approx-
imation algorithms for this problem, i.e., algorithms that compute a feasible solution S whose cost is
provably within some factor α > 1 of opt (the approximation factor or approximation ratio/guarantee).
    Most of the best-known approximation algorithms for k-median, including the current-best one
[CGLS23, GPST23], are based on the following high-level approach (see Section 1.2 for alternative
approaches in the literature). One considers the associated facility location problem, where instead of
k we are given a (uniform) facility opening cost f . In this problem a feasible solution S can contain an
arbitrary number of centers/facilities (rather than k), however in the objective function, besides the
connection cost cost(S), one has to pay also the opening cost f |S|. Then one uses a Lagrangian Multiplier
Preserving (LMP) α LMP -approximation for the problem (formal definition in Section 1.3) to construct
a bi-point solution for k-median, i.e., a convex combination of a solution S1 (opening fewer than k
centers) and a solution S2 (opening more than k centers), that opens exactly k centers in a fractional
sense. In particular, sampling one of the two solutions according to the respective coefficients would
give a solution of expected cost α LMP · opt that opens k centers in expectation. Finally, a bi-point
rounding algorithm is used to derive a feasible k-median solution, while losing another factor α BPR
in the approximation. The product α LMP · α BPR gives the overall approximation factor. We remark
that the state of the art leaves a significant gap between the approximation factor for a solution with
k centers in expectation and a true k-median solution.


        Reference             Approximation factor                             Technique
          [Bar96]               O(log n log log n)                         tree embeddings
        [CCGG98]                O(log k log log k )                        tree embeddings
         [CGTS99]                     6.667                            dependent LP rounding
           [JV01]                       6                              LMP + bi-point rounding
     [JMS02, JMM+ 03]                   4                              LMP + bi-point rounding
        [AGK+ 04]                     3+ε                                     local search
           [LS16]                     2.733                            LMP + bi-point rounding
         [BPR+ 17]                    2.675                            LMP + bi-point rounding
    [CGLS23, GPST23]                  2.613                            LMP + bi-point rounding
       Theorem 1.1                    2+ε                         LMP interpolation + stable algorithm

Table 1: Progression of approximation factors for k-median. Dependencies on ε are omitted due to
numerical overestimation, except for [AGK+ 04] and the final result.

   In more detail, for the LMP stage, [JV01] gave a 3-approximation algorithm which was subse-
quently improved to 2 by [JMS02, JMM+ 03], and then to 2 − η for some η > 2.25 · 10−7 by [CGLS23].
    1 Sometimes in the literature one assumes F = D or D ⊆ F. We consider the more general case and make no such assump-

tion.

a polynomial-time algorithm for obtaining a proper k-median clustering from a k + O(1) cluster-
ing essentially without any loss in the approximation ratio. This approach was further refined by
[BPR+ 17] who gave a 1.337-approximate bi-point√

better than ϕ ≈ 1.272, where ϕ = 1+2 5 is the golden ratio.
    Unfortunately, there is an inherent limitation to this approach. No approximation algorithm for
the LMP stage can do better than 1 + 2/e [JMS02, JMM+ 03], and using the lower bound for bi-point
rounding algorithms√by [GPST23], the best possible analysis cannot yield an approximation factor
better than (1 + 2/e) ϕ ≈ 2.207. This leaves a significant gap to the inapproximability lower bound
of (1 + 2/e) ≈ 1.735 [JMS02, JMM+ 03], and even to the best-known approximation algorithm that
opens k-centers in expectation [CGLS23], which achieves an approximation guarantee of 2 − η for
some small η > 0.

1.1   Our Results
Our main result is stated as follows. (As commonly done, we use ”with high probability” to mean
that an event occurs with probability at least 1 − 1/poly(n).)
Theorem 1.1. For every ε > 0, there is a randomized polynomial-time algorithm for k-median that returns a
solution with cost at most (2 + ε)opt with high probability.
    The algorithm in our main theorem combines two novel algorithms. First, we address the lim-
itations of the bi-point rounding method by introducing a non-trivial variant of the classic Greedy
algorithm [JMS02, JMM+ 03]. The Greedy algorithm can be highly adaptive in that tiny changes to the
opening cost f may lead to vastly different solutions S and S′ , where |S| < k and |S′ | > k, forcing pre-
vious bi-point rounding approaches to incur a large additional factor in the approximation guarantee.
We resolve this high-adaptivity by presenting a low-adaptivity version, which makes adaptive deci-
sions in only O(log(n)/ε2 ) phases. This modification intuitively renders the algorithm less adaptive
than the original, where each choice depends heavily on previous ones. We then introduce a novel
walk-between-solutions algorithm that enables us to return a solution with at most k + O(log(n)/ε2 )
centers, with no additional loss in the approximation factor (up to 1 + ϵ). Specifically, we achieve the
following result.
Theorem 1.2. For every constant ε ∈ (0, 1/6), there is a polynomial-time algorithm for k-median that returns
a solution containing at most k + O(log n/ε2 ) many centers and of cost at most (2 + ε)opt.
    Theorem 1.2 is proved in Section 3, and an overview of the main ideas is given in Section 2.1.
    Combining this result with the [LS16] reduction from k-median pseudo-solutions to true k-median
solutions already yields a (2 + ε)-approximation in quasi-polynomial time. In order to improve this
to a polynomial time algorithm, we use a completely different algorithm. We say that an instance is
β-stable if optk−1 ≥ (1 + β) · optk . Our result is as follows.
Theorem 1.3. For any two constants ε, ζ > 0, there exists a randomized polynomial-time algorithm that, given
a (ζ/ log n)-stable k-median instance, returns a solution of cost at most (2 + ε)opt with high probability.
   The algorithm for stable instances is inspired by a recent fixed-parameter-tractable approach that
essentially identifies, by enumeration, small-radius balls guaranteed to contain the optimal centers
and then solves the problem by maximizing a submodular function subject to a partition matroid
constraint. However, there are two major challenges: first, the running time of [CGK+ 19] is not
polynomial even when k = O(log n); and second, because k may be large (potentially polynomial
in n), we need to identify almost all clusters of the optimal solution before proceeding with any
guessing/enumeration steps. We address these challenges through several novel ideas, including a
sampling approach based on the k-means++ algorithm. The proof of Theorem 1.3 is presented in
Section 4, with an overview of the techniques provided in Section 2.2.
  2 Solutions opening slightly more than k centers are sometimes called pseudo-solutions in the literature.




                                                               2
    Let us see why Theorems 1.2 and 1.3 combine to yield Theorem 1.1. We compute a few feasible
solutions. Let ∆ be the surplus number of centers used in Theorem 1.2. Let k′ = max{1, k − ∆}. One
solution is obtained by computing the optimum solution with one center for k′ = 1, and otherwise
by running the algorithm from Theorem 1.2 with the number of centers being k′ = k − ∆. If optk′ ≤
optk · (1 + ε), this is a good enough approximation. Then we run the algorithm from Theorem 1.3
for every k′′ ∈ (k′ , k ]. If the solution with k′ centers computed before is not good enough, then there
exists some k′′ ∈ (k′ , k ] such that optk′′ −1 ≥ (1 + β) · optk′′ with β ∈ Ω(ε/∆) = Ω(ε3 / log n) and
optk′′ ≤ (1 + ε) · optk . In this case, Theorem 1.3 yields the desired result for a proper choice of the
parameters. Outputting the best of all computed solutions yields Theorem 1.1.
    For Euclidean spaces, the set of candidate centers is infinite. By a standard reduction, we can find a
polynomial-sized set F ⊂ Rd in polynomial time such that the cost of any constant factor approximate
solution is approximated by a set of centers obtained from F, see for example Lemma 5.3 of [KSS10].
Thus we also obtain a (2 + ε)-approximation in Euclidean spaces, improving on 2.406 [CEMN22].
    Finally, let us remark that Theorem 1.1 essentially closes the gap between the approximation guar-
antee of the best-known algorithm that opens k centers in expectation [CGLS23] and algorithms that
consistently open at most k centers. However, a major open problem remains: obtaining a tight ap-
proximation result for k-median, as it is only known that it is hard to approximate the problem within
a factor less than (1 + 2/e) [JMS02]. Building on this work, a natural next step would be to resolve
this question or to significantly improve the approximation guarantee, even in the setting where k
centers are opened in expectation.

1.2   Related Work
We already reviewed the LMP-based approximation algorithms earlier. But there are several other
important results for k-median and closely related problems which we cover here.
    There also exist other approaches for designing approximation algorithms for metric k-median,
most notably local search [AGK+ 04, CGH+ 22, GT08, KMN+ 04], combinatorial algorithms [MP03],
metric embeddings [Bar98, Bar96, CCGG98], or other LP-based rounding approaches [ARS03, CGTS02,
CL12]. There has also been a lot of work for the related problems k-means and k-center, which mini-
mize the sum of squared distances and the maximum distance, respectively. Specifically for k-center,
[Gon85, HS86] gave a 2-approximation which is optimal unless P ̸= NP. k-means admits a (9 + ε)-
approximation in general metrics [ANSW20]. In addition, both k-median and k-means have been
studied in d-dimensional Euclidean spaces, where a PTAS is possible when either d or k are fixed
[ARR98, KSS10, FRS19, CFS19]. If neither d nor k are fixed, the approximation factors for Euclidean
k-median and k-means are also better than their general metric counterparts [ANSW20, GOR+ 22],
with the best known approximation ratio being 5.913 for k-means and, prior to our work, being 2.406
for k-median by [CEMN22]. Both in high dimensional Euclidean spaces as well as general metrics, all
of these aforementioned problems are APX hard [ACKS15, CCSL21, CK19, GI03, JMS02, LSW17].
    LMP-based approaches are also very relevant for the related facility location problem. Mahdian
et al. [MYZ06] showed that an LMP (1 + 2/e)-approximation yields a (γGK ≈ 1.463)-approximation
for UFL, which matches the hardness result of Guha and Khuller [GK99] where γGK is the solution
of γ = 1 + 2e−γ . Nevertheless, most improvements do not rely on an LMP approach. Following a
long line of research [BA10, CG05, CS03, JMM+ 03, JMS02, KPR00, MYZ06, STA97], Li presented an
algorithm with the currently best known approximation ratio of roughly 1.488 [Li13].

1.3   Preliminaries
We are given a set of n clients D and set of m potential centers or facilities F. Given a set S ⊆ F, we
use d( j, S) = mini∈S d( j, i ), where d is the underlying distance function. Õ( x ) suppresses polylog( x )
factors, and Oε ( x ) suppresses polynomial factors in ε. We use [ a]+ = max( a, 0). We already defined
the k-median problem. The uncapacitated facility location problem (UFL) with uniform opening
costs is given a non-negative number f and asks to find a set S ⊆ F minimizing
                                    costUFL (S) = ∑ d( j, S) + f · |S|.
                                                    j∈ D

We refer to ∑ j∈ D d( j, S) as the connection cost and f · |S| as the opening cost. Let xi,j denote in the
indicator variable for serving client j with facility i and let yi be the indicator variable for opening


                                                      3
facility i. Relaxing these to continuous variables in [0, 1], the LP relaxation to k-median and UFL are
given by the following equations:



      min      ∑         d(i, j) xi,j                ( LPkm )
            i ∈ F,j∈ D                                                   min      ∑         d(i, j) xi,j + f · ∑ yi    ( LPUFL ( f ))
       s.t. ∑ xi,j ≥ 1                               ∀j ∈ D                    i ∈ F,j∈ D                    i∈ F
            i∈ F                                                          s.t. ∑ xi,j ≥ 1                                   ∀j ∈ D
            yi − xi,j ≥ 0                      ∀ j ∈ D, i ∈ F                  i∈ F

            ∑ yi ≤ k                                                           yi − xi,j ≥ 0                          ∀ j ∈ D, i ∈ F

            x, y ≥ 0



    The constraint ∑i∈ F xi,j ≥ 1 ensures that every client is served by some facility and the constraint
yi − xi,j ≥ 0 ensures that a client j can only be served by facility i if i is open. Moreover, the dual for
the facility location LP relaxation is given by the equations:

                               max ∑ α j                                                       ( DPUFL ( f ))
                                        j∈ D

                                 s.t. ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ ≤ f                                          ∀i ∈ F
                                        j∈ D

                                        α≥0

The usual interpretation of the α j is that they are the budgets for each client, accounting for both
connecting the client to a facility, as well as opening a facility. The opening contribution dual variables
β i,j obtained from the yi − xi,j ≥ 0 constraints, as well as the dual constraints α j − β i,j ≤ d(i, j) are
removed here, as we can always set β i,j = [α j − d(i, j)]+ . Recall that opt = optk is the cost of an
optimal k-median clustering. We use opt LP and opt LP ( f ) to denote the cost of an optimal solution to
LPkm and LPUFL ( f ), resp. Trivially opt ≥ opt LP . Since any feasible solution for LPkm is also feasible
for LPUFL ( f ), one has opt LP ≥ opt LP ( f ) − k f . We will use the fact that, by weak duality, any feasible
solution to DPUFL ( f ) induces a lower bound on opt LP ( f ). In particular, for any feasible solution α to
DPUFL ( f ), we have opt ≥ opt LP ( f ) − k f ≥ ∑ j∈ D α j − k f .
      We say that an algorithm is a Lagrangian Multiplier Preserving (LMP) α-approximation algorithm
for UFL if it returns a solution S with

                                                   cost(S) ≤ α · (opt LP ( f ) − f |S|).

    Finally, we will make the assumption that the distances are integers in [1, n3 /ε]. The justification
is given by the following standard reduction.
Lemma 1.4. For any constants ε > 0 and α > 1, given a polynomial-time α-approximation algorithm for k-
median on instances with distances in {1, . . . , n3 /ε}, there exists a polynomial-time α(1 + O(ε))-approximation
algorithm for k-median on general instances.
Proof. Consider any input instance ( D ∪ F, d) of k-median. Assume that n := | D | is large enough
w.r.t. α, otherwise the problem can be solved by brute force in polynomial time. We guess M :=
max j∈ D d( j, OPT), where OPT is some optimal k-median solution, by trying all the n · m possibilities.
                                                                                              d(i,j) n
Next, for each (i, j) ∈ F × D, define d′ ( j, i ) = d′ (i, j) = max{1, ⌈ M                           ε ⌉} if d (i, j ) ≤ M. Set all the
                                               3
remaining d′ ( a, b), a ̸= b, to nε . Finally, replace the d′ (i, j)’s with the corresponding metric closure.
We run the given α-approximation algorithm on the k-median instance ( D ∪ F, d′ ), hence obtaining a
solution S. It is easy to check that S is a good enough approximation for the input instance.




                                                                    4
2     Technical Overview
In this section, we give an overview of our result. Section 2.1 introduces ideas behind Theorem 1.2 that
achieves a (2 + ε)-approximation while opening k + O(log n/ε2 ) centers, and Section 2.2 discusses
Theorem 1.3 for stable instances.

2.1     (2 + ε)-Approximation with Oε (log n) Extra Centers
Let us motivate our high-level plan by revisiting the previous framework. The previous best approxi-
mation algorithms [LS13, BPR+ 17, GPST23, CGLS23] for k-median start by the following initialization
step: starting from two LMP approximate solutions for UFL S, S′ ⊆ F with the respective facility costs
f , f ′ such that |S| < k, |S′ | > k3 and f possibly much larger than f ′ , the algorithm uses binary search
to obtain two new solutions (S, f ), (S′ , f ′ ) such that |S| < k, |S′ | > k and | f − f ′ | ≤ 2−n . This step
can be already viewed as a merging step that takes two solutions whose number of facilities sandwich
k and makes them (specifically the facility cost) closer. Then, the natural question is can we merge S
and S′ into the (almost) same set of open facilities so that both open (almost) exactly k facilities?
      One naive implementation of this idea might be the following (from now on, let us assume f = f ′ ).
Just like binary search, suppose that we find an LMP approximate solution S′′ which is strictly between
S and S′ in some formal sense. If |S′′ | > k, we will let S′ ← S′′ and otherwise S ← S′′ . Then the
invariant |S| < k, |S′ | > k is maintained while the two solutions got strictly closer. So we can continue
this process until they become the same!
      Of course, the main question is whether one can find such S′′ given S and S′ . Just like S and S′ , S′′
must be an outcome of the LMP approximation algorithm, or at least close to such an outcome in some
formal sense. Since being a valid outcome of an algorithm is a highly complex property, especially when
the algorithm has a large number of adaptive steps whose action depends on the previous actions, this
task remains challenging for a general LMP approximation algorithm.
      However, there is an example of success for Jain and Vazirani’s primal-dual [JV01] algorithm,
which gives a LMP 3-approximation. At a high level, this algorithm consists of two phases, where
the first phase yields a set T of tentative open facilities, and the second phase is allowed to choose an
arbitrary S ⊆ T that forms a maximal independent set in an auxiliary graph, so there is a freedom
in choosing the independent set. Ahmadian et al. [ANSW20] used this freedom to give a walking
procedure that finds a maximal independent set in the auxiliary graph of cardinality exactly k, which
led to an improved approximation ratio for Euclidean k-means/k-median and metric k-means.
      However, the Greedy algorithm [JMS02, JMM+ 03] with dual-fitting analysis, which guarantees a
better 2-LMP approximation, is more adaptive; in fact, it can be a completely deterministic algorithm
without leaving any freedom. Therefore, in order to implement our idea, it would be better to find a
low-adaptive version of the Greedy algorithm, where the algorithm runs in a small (Oε (log n) in our
case) number of phases, and each phase presents some freedom of choices for the algorithm.

The Greedy Algorithm. Let us recall the Greedy algorithm [JMS02, JMM+ 03]. The algorithm main-
tains the set A of active clients and the set S of open facilities. Initially, all clients are active, i.e., A = D,
and no facilities are opened, i.e., S = ∅. Additionally, the algorithm maintains dual variables (α j ) j∈ D ,
initialized to 0.
    While there is at least one active client, the algorithm uniformly increases the α-values of the active
clients until there is a facility i whose ”bids” are sufficient to open it (or an active client becomes
“close” to an already opened facility). The bids from a client j to a facility i depend on whether j
is active or not. If j ∈ A, then a portion of its budget α j , specifically d(i, j), is allocated to cover
the connection cost, and the remainder, α j − d(i, j), is used to bid towards the facility’s opening cost
fˆ := 2 f . For a client j ∈
                           / A, which is already connected to an open facility at a distance d( j, S), the bid
towards i is the difference in distance, i.e., d( j, S) − d(i, j) (or 0 if i is farther). Finally, the algorithm
marks any client j with α j ≥ d( j, S) as inactive, ensuring that at any point in time, active clients j
satisfy α j ≤ d( j, S).
    In order to prove that the algorithm yields an LMP 2-approximation, let (α∗j ) j∈ D be the final α
values and S∗ be the set of opened facilities. The design of the algorithm guarantees that the α∗
    3 Throughout this overview, we assume no solution opens exactly k centers, because we are done if this happens.




                                                              5
values pay for both the opening costs of the facilities in S∗ and the connection costs of the clients,
leading to ∑ j∈ D α∗j = ∑ j∈ D d( j, S∗ ) + fˆ · |S∗ |.
    The more interesting part of the analysis is to show that α∗ /2 is a dual feasible solution for the
dual facility location LP with facility cost f , namely ∑ j∈ D [α∗j − 2d(i, j)]+ ≤ fˆ for every facility i. The
dual feasibility follows from two key properties that, at any point in time, (a) α j ≤ d( j, S) for every
j ∈ A and (b) for every i ∈ F, no facility is overbid:

                                  ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ .                              (1)
                                  j∈ A                 j∈ D \ A


2.1.1   Logarithmic Adaptivity
As stated, the G REEDY is a highly adaptive algorithm; for instance, consider the case when two facili-
ties i and i′ both become paid for at the same time θ. Whether we open i or i′ can dramatically change
the bids to future facilities and completely change the trace of the algorithm.
     To reduce such high adaptivity, we limit the number of time steps where such decisions are made.
Specifically, our high-level goal is to only consider the time steps of the form (1 + ε2 )0 , (1 + ε2 )1 , (1 +
ε2 )2 , . . .. Then, as the minimum nonzero distance is 1 and the maximum distance is poly(n), the total
number of time steps that we need to consider is O(log n/ε2 ), and this is what we refer to as O(log n)
adaptivity.
     The main difficulty in implementing this idea is to ensure (1). Consider the situation where all
active clients have α value θ = (1 + ε2 )t . If their α values jump from θ to (1 + ε2 )θ, it is possible that a
previously underbid facility becomes strictly overbid.
     We fix this issue in two steps. The first easy fix is to scale down the metric by a factor of (1 − δ)
where δ = O(ε); that is, we consider a facility i paid for if

                       ∑ [α j − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ [d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≥ fˆ .                          (2)
                       j∈ A                                       j∈ D \ A

At the cost of losing a factor 1/(1 − δ) in the approximation guarantee, this has the following benefit.
If i is not paid according to (2) when all active clients have α j = θ, then even when α j ’s are increased
to (1 + ε2 )θ, for a client j with d( j, i ) ≥ εθ, its contribution [(1 + ε2 )t+1 − d(i, j)]+ to (1) is at most its
previous contribution [(1 + ε2 )t − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ to (2), which makes (1) unlikely to be violated.
     Then our second fix deals with active clients close to co-located with i. We do so by allowing the
clients j in the ball B(i, εθ ) to increase their bids for i up to min((1 + ε2 )θ, (1 − δ)d( j, S)), so that if i
could not be opened even with increased bids from nearby clients, then increasing active clients’ α
values from θ to (1 + ε2 )θ should not make i overbid. These increased bids bring other considera-
tions (e.g., ensuring dual feasibility of other facilities), leading to the following definition of openable
facilities, which can be efficiently checked by a linear program.
Definition 1. A facility i is openable with respect to the algorithm’s state (α, S, A, θ ) if there are bids (τj ) j∈ A
of active clients that satisfy the following conditions.
   • Only nearby clients are increased:
                                           τj = α j     for every j ∈ A − B(i, εθ ).

   • Nearby clients are only increased slightly:

                      α j ≤ τj ≤ min{(1 − δ)d( j, S), (1 + ε2 )θ }              for every j ∈ A ∩ B(i, εθ ).

   • Facility i is paid for:

                              ∑ [τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ [d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+ ≥ fˆ .
                          j∈ A                                       j∈ D \ A

   • Dual feasibility: for every facility i0 and ℓ ∈ A,

                                 ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [τℓ − 2d( j, i0 ) − d(ℓ, i0 )]+ ≤ fˆ.
                                 j∈ A                 j∈ D \ A


                                                            6
  Algorithm 1 (L OG A DAPTIVE).
  Initialization: Set fˆ = 2 f , S = ∅, A = D, θ = 1 and α j = θ for every j ∈ A = D.

  Repeat the following while A ̸= ∅:

        1. While there is an unopened facility i that is openable (chosen arbitrarily if there are many),
                Calculate (τj ) j∈ A so that i is openable with bids (τj ) j∈ A . Open i (i.e., add it to S),
                let α j ← τj for every j ∈ A (note that only the values of the clients in A ∩ B(i, εθ )
                change), mark all clients j ∈ A such that α j ≥ (1 − δ)d( j, S) as served (i.e., remove
                them from A).
        2. Perform the following step and proceed to the next phase.
                For every remaining j ∈ A, let α j ← min((1 + ε2 )θ, (1 − δ)d( j, S)), remove it from
                A when α j = (1 − δ)d( j, S). Update θ = (1 + ε2 )θ.




    Given this definition of openability, Section 3.2 presents our modified Greedy algorithm with
O(log n) phases, L OG A DAPTIVE, which is restated here as Algorithm 1. Here a phase is defined to
be the run of the algorithm for a fixed value of θ = 1, (1 + ε2 ), (1 + ε2 )2 , . . . , which is broken down
to two stages according to the description of Algorithm 1. Note that at least in the beginning, stage 1
allows opening of any openable facility, providing the desired freedom to walk between two solutions
within the phase. While stage 1 still presents some adaptivity as an opening of a facility might impact
the openability of another, it is sufficient for the merging procedure described in Section 2.1.2.
    For the analysis, in Section 3.3, we prove that L OG A DAPTIVE yields an LMP (2 + O(ε))-approximate
solution for UFL. It is a nontrivial extension of the dual-fitting analysis of the Greedy algorithm for
our definition of openability and design of the algorithm.

2.1.2    Merging Two Solutions
We describe our final algorithm M ERGE S OLUTIONS that maintains two LMP approximate solutions
whose number of facilities sandwich k and gradually merges them until one of them opens almost
exactly k facilities.

Setup. Based on the discussion about L OG A DAPTIVE, we will describe a solution H as ( H1 , . . . , HL )
with L = O(log n/ε2 ), where Hi is a valid execution of stage 1 of phase i (i.e., when θ = (1 + ε2 )i−1 ),
which is formally represented as a sequence of opened facilities. Hi ’s validity also requires maximality,
meaning that no openable facility remains in the phase. In order to facilitate the merging, given a
phase, we extend the notion of a valid execution to include the following actions.

   • We allow opening a free facility, at most three times per phase. A free facility ĩ is based on a
     regular facility i ∈ F, and when it is created, we can choose the distance parameter u(ĩ ) = d(ĩ, i ).
     All other distances d(ĩ, x ) are defined to be u(ĩ ) + d(i, x ).

   • We slightly relax the notion of openability so that one can open a facility i by paying fˆ − nη
     instead of fˆ for parameter η := 2−n (in the third bullet of Definition 1).

    Call H = ( H1 , . . . , HL ) a solution of η-valid sequences if Hi is a valid execution (i.e., an η-valid
sequence) of the ith phase in the above relaxed notion given H1 , . . . , Hi−1 . We extend the analysis
of L OG A DAPTIVE in Appendix A to show that such H in the above sense still yields an LMP (2 +
O(ε + nη ))-approximation, if we do not count the opening cost of the free facilities (while using the
connections they provide). Therefore, a solution of η-valid sequences that opens exactly k regular
facilities, and at most 3 free facilities per phase, is a (2 + O(ε + nη ))-approximation to k-median that
opens at most O( L) = O(log n/ε2 ) extra facilities.


                                                          7
                i2                        i2                         i2                              i2                         i2
      i3                  i6    i3                   i6    i3                   i6         i3                   i6    i3                  i6
                i1                        ĩ1                        ĩ1             ...             ĩ1                        i1
                           Step 1                     Step 2                                                     Step 3
           i4        i5              i4         i5              i4         i5                   i4         i5              i4        i5

Figure 1: How we walk from H p′ to H p . Dark grey color denotes a free facility and light grey color
denotes regular open facilities.


      Initially, by the standard binary search on the facility cost and running L OG A DAPTIVE, we start
with two solutions H = ( H1 , . . . , HL ) and H ′ = ( H1′ , . . . , HL′ ) with facility costs f and f ′ such that | f −
f ′ | ≤ η and their numbers of opened facilities sandwich k. (We will associate parameters ( f , u) for
H and ( f ′ , u′ ) for H′ . Also, note that every facility in H (resp. H′ ) has cost f (resp. f ′ ).) Throughout
the algorithm, we will maintain the invariants that (1) H and H ′ open the same set of free facilities
(so that u and u′ are defined on the same set), (2) their number of opened regular facilities sandwich
k, and (3) ( f , u) = ( f ′ , u′ ) except for one parameter f or u(ĩ ) for one free ĩ, whose values in the two
solutions differ by at most η. Call such a parameter the difference parameter.
      For each i = 1, . . . , L, we will outline a procedure that, given that the prefixes ( H1 , . . . , H p−1 ) and
( H1′ , . . . , H p′ −1 ) are identical, modifies the suffixes ( H p , . . . , HL ) and ( H p′ , . . . , HL′ ) such that H p and H p′
become identical while satisfying the above invariants, unless we already find a solution that opens
exactly k regular facilities. This will allow us to gradually merge the solution until one of them opens
exactly k regular facilities (and at most O( L) free facilities).

How to walk in each phase.                Finally, we focus on phase p and introduce ideas behind ensuring
H p = H p′ . Our first step is to change the difference parameter value of H ′ to that of H; formally,
we let H ′′ = C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( f ,u) ( H1′ , . . . , H p′ ), which is the solution resulting from running
L OG A DAPTIVE from a given partial solution ( H1′ , . . . , H p′ ) with parameters ( f , u) instead of ( f ′ , u′ ).
Such a change of the difference parameter is the reason for introducing the nη slack for η-valid se-
quences; for instance, if Hi was the exact execution with parameters ( f ′ , u′ ), our Claim 2 shows that
it is still η-valid with respect to ( f , u).
      If H′′ opens k regular facilities we are done. Also, if the number of open regular facilities of H′′
and H ′ sandwich k, then we already made progress as they now coincide on the pth phase while
still having one difference parameter. Therefore, we only need to handle the case where H ′′ and H
sandwich k, where the difference parameters already became identical but H p and H p′ are different.
Let us rename H′′ to H′ .
      Now our goal is to gradually change H p′ to H p (i.e., walk from H p′ to H p ). Instead of describing the
full procedure, which is outlined in Section 3.5, we illustrate the procedure on a particular example.
Suppose that H p = ⟨i1 ⟩, H p′ = ⟨i2 , . . . , i6 ⟩ where one can open at most one between i1 and i j for
any j ∈ {2, . . . , 6}; this constraint can be expressed as a star graph with i1 as the center vertex and
i2 , . . . , i6 are only connected to i1 , and a valid solution (i.e., η-valid sequence) here means a maximal
independent set. (Suppose that this is the only constraint on openings, so H p and H p′ can be thought
of as sets instead of sequences.)
      In this example, H p and H p′ are the only two maximal independent sets, so it is impossible to
smoothly walk from H p′ to H p . Here is where the notion of a free facility becomes useful because we
can assume that having a free facility helps maximality while not hurting independence. Then, from H p′ ,
by (Step 1) adding a free copy of i1 called ĩ1 with u(ĩ1 ) = 0, (Step 2) removing i2 , . . . , i6 one-by-one,
and (Step 3) converting ĩ1 to a regular i1 , we obtain a sequence of maximal independent sets where a
consecutive pair of independent sets differ by at most one facility. Figure 1 illustrates this process.
      For each intermediate H p′ , we consider its completion (i.e., C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( f ,u) ( H1′ , . . . , H p′ ))
and see how many regular facilities it opens. Since the completions from the initial H p′ and H p sand-
wich k, there must be a consecutive pair in the above sequence of independent sets (say H pa and H pb )
that sandwich k, and they differ by only one facility iq (say H pa contains iq while H pb does not). For
future execution of the algorithm, H pa and H pb are the same as the solutions that have the free copy ĩq


                                                                     8
(instead of iq ) with the u(ĩq ) value 0 (for H pa ) and ∞ (or sufficiently large number, for H pb ). Therefore,
by performing binary search on the u(ĩq ), just like we do on the facility cost f , we yield two solutions
that are now identical up to phase p except the η difference on the only one different parameter u(ĩq ),
which archives our goal of merging two solutions at phase p!
    In general, the fact that H p and H p′ are not just sets but sequences of opened facilities requires
more technical consideration. However, the full procedure is still an extension of this idea, trying
to increase the length of the common prefix between H p and H p′ by walking from H p′ to H p by one
coordinate at a time, until two consecutive solutions are sandwiching k.

2.2     (2 + ε)-Approximation for Oε ( log1 n )-Stable Instances
The other component of our algorithm is an improved approximation to k-median assuming that
the instance has a weak version of a structural property known as stability, initially formulated by
[ORSS12]. Recall that an instance is β-stable if optk−1 ≥ (1 + β)optk . For any β-stable instance,
                                                                                               −1 −O(1)
[ABS10, CS17] showed how to obtain a PTAS for the problem in time n β ε                and [ABS10] left as
an open question as whether one could obtain a PTAS with running time f ( β, ε)nOε (1) for any com-
putable function f for general metrics (they show it is possible for Euclidean metrics). We would in-
deed like to have an algorithm running in time 2poly( β,ε) nOε (1) to solve our (ε/ log n)-stable instances.
    Unfortunately, since any k-median instance is Ω(1/k )-stable, obtaining a PTAS with the above
running time would violate Gap-ETH [CGK+ 19]: The k-median problem cannot be approximated
better than 1 + 2/e in time f (k, ε)nOε (1) assuming Gap-ETH.
    The saving grace here is that we are not after a (1 + ε)-approximation algorithm but we can use
any (2 + O(ε))-approximation with the above running time. Since a connection between β-stable
and FPT algorithm exists (as explained in the above discussion), it is tempting to make the journey
from FPT algorithms back to β-stable instances and use the state-of-the-art approximation algorithm
of [CGK+ 19] to solve β-stable instances. Unfortunately the algorithm presented in [CGK+ 19] runs in
time (log n)Õ(k/ε) nO(1) and so importing the techniques directly would result in a running time of the
                     −1
form (log n)Õ(( β/ε) ) nO(1) and not to a polynomial running time for our setting. We must thus go
beyond the state-of-the-art techniques for FPT and β-stable k-median instances.

2.2.1    Local Optima and Stability
One particularly important tool when dealing with stable solutions are properties of local optima.
The local search procedure starts with any feasible solution S, and searches for an alternative solution
S′ such that |S△S′ | = 2 (i.e., S and S′ differ by one center) and cost(S′ ) < cost(S). If such an S′ exists,
S is replaced by S′ and the process is iterated. At the end of the procedure we say that S is locally
optimal. The running time of this procedure is polynomial, where we exploit the assumption that
distances are polynomially bounded integers (by Lemma 1.4).
    We first introduce a bit of notation. Suppose we are given a locally optimal solution S. We say that
a cluster C ∗ of the optimal solution OPT is pure with respect to S, if there exists a cluster C ′ induced by
S such that both clusters agree on all but O(ε) · min(|C ∗ |, |C ′ |) of the points. We begin by examining
structural properties for β-stable solutions, summarized by in Section 4.1.

   • For any given constant-factor approximation S, at most Oε ( β−1 ) many clusters are not pure, see
     Lemma 4.3.
   • In any locally optimal solution S, we have

                                  ∑            d( p, S) ≤           ∑          d( p, OPT) + O(ε) · opt,
                            p∈C ∗ , C ∗ pure                p∈C ∗ , C ∗ pure

        that is the cost of the clients in pure clusters is well approximated.
More precisely, in the second bullet we show that all the points in each pure cluster C ∗ with center c∗
can be assigned to the same center t(c∗ ) in S (i.e., the center c′ of C ′ ) with a moderate increase of their
cost (see Lemma 4.4).



                                                                9
    The first property relies on the fact that for every non-pure cluster, S must merge at least two clus-
ters of the optimum. If S is a constant-factor approximation, this cannot happen more than Oε ( β−1 )
many times before the approximation guarantee no longer holds. The second property is implied by
the local optimality of S. Specifically, swapping out the center c′ ∈ S serving C ′ for the center c∗ of the
pure cluster C ∗ cannot change the cost by much as these two clusters agree on most of their points.
    In a nutshell, a locally optimal solution S approximates all but Oε ( β−1 ) many clusters of the
optimal solution very well. In what follows, we will describe how we may extend S to a (2 + ε)-
approximation overall.

2.2.2   Identifying the Non-Pure Clusters
To find a good approximation for the non-pure clusters, we would like to identify these clusters. By
the above discussion, we may assume that we have a locally optimal solution S for which all but
Oε ( β−1 ) = Oε (log n) many clusters are well approximated. Immediate ideas, such as determining
the badly approximated clusters by brute force, will not improve over the quasi-polynomial running
time. Instead, we look towards fixed parameter tractable (FPT) approaches, parameterized by the
number of centers. In fact, we would like an FPT approximation algorithm parameterized by the number
of non-pure clusters. Starting point is an FPT algorithm based on submodular maximization, initially
due to [CGK+ 19], which we first review. However, recall that the running time of this algorithm is
            −1
(log k)Õ(kε ) and so will not be enough for our purpose. We thus introduce new ideas to speed things
up.

Review of the FPT approximation algorithm of [CGK+ 19]. Suppose c∗ is a center of cluster C ∗ in
an optimal solution OPT. [CGK+ 19] introduce the notion of a leader client ℓ ∈ C ∗ which minimizes the
distance to c∗ . Using coresets to first reduce the number of clients to Õ(k log n/ε2 ), the leaders of all
                                                                                                     −1
clusters, as well as the distances d(ℓ, c∗ ) can be identified in FPT-time, i.e. in time (log n)Õ(kε ) · nO(1) .
A solution S obtained by simply selecting an arbitrary facility within distance d(ℓ, c∗ ) from ℓ yields a
3-approximation. [CGK+ 19] improve upon this by showing that for any two candidate sets of centers
S′ and Λ

is a monotone submodular function.
    The first part which consists in guessing the leaders is the computational bottleneck since opti-
mizing a monotone submodular function can be done in polynomial time.
    We next describe how we deal with the clusters that are not well approximated. Recall that we
first compute a locally optimal solution S. By the properties of local optima, S is already a sufficiently
good solution for the pure clusters. For the remaining Oε (log n) non-pure clusters, we only have
coarser approximation bounds.

proportionately to cost(S) . With probability εO(1) / log n, we are picking a leader from a given non-

clusters. Thereafter, we can show that with good probability, we can assign all the points of a non-
pure non-basic-cheap non-hit cluster C ∗ , with center c∗ , to the center t(c∗ ) ∈ S which is closest to c∗
with a small cost (see Lemma 4.5 in Section 4.2). We next call expensive the non-pure non-basic-cheap
clusters which are hit, and cheap the non-pure clusters which are either basic-cheap or non-basic-
cheap and non-hit. Thus the clusters are partitioned into pure, cheap and expensive ones. Since we
have sampled Oε (log n) clients, we can enumerate all the subsets in polynomial time and focus on a
subset that contains exactly one leader of each expensive cluster.
    Using a constant number of potential guesses per leader, we then show how to get a sufficiently
good approximation of the distance ρ from each leader to its closest optimum center, see Lemma 4.8
in Section 4.3. Intuitively, the cost of the leader in solution S is a rough approximation of ρ, so we can
interpolate around it.

and guessed distance/radii ρ, we will introduce a dummy center at distance ρ from ℓ and at distance
d( p, ℓ) + ρ from any other vertex p.
                                                                                              ˆ does
    If we promise to open a center in the ball of radii ρ around ℓ – which solution S − S0 ∪ OPT
– then we will guarantee that there will be a center not further away than the dummy centers in
the final solution. We now work with this solution S ∪ Λ which provides a worst-case fall back on
where the centers in OPTˆ could be. We can show that MD := S ∪ Λ − S0 is a (3 + O(ε))-approximate
solution.


                                                                11
     Equipped with this we proceed to our 3-step center removal. We know that we have at most
|S0 | = Oε (log n) centers we need to remove. Our first procedure (see Section 4.4) aims at removing
the centers of clusters of high cost in S0 . Start with solution S ∪ Λ. For Oε (log n) times, we sample a
center c ∈ S proportionally to the cost of its cluster in solution S ∪ Λ, remove c from S with probability
1/2, and repeat on the resulting solution. Let Q be the centers removed from S. Then with probability
at least 2−Oε (log n) the procedure succeeds by either obtaining Q = S0 or ensuring that the total cost
of the clusters of S0 − Q in the solution is at most O(ε · opt). This indeed follows from the fact that,
if we only removed centers Q ⊆ S0 up to a certain step, the solution is never more than a (3 + O(ε))-
approximation (since S ∪ Λ − S0 is a (3 + O(ε))-approximation) and so the probability of sampling
a new center of S0 − Q is Ω(ε), unless the total cost of the clusters of S0 − Q is small. Therefore,
repeating the process nOε (1) times, we have that one of the runs succeeds with high probability.
     Thus, after this step, we have removed a set Q of centers of S0 and the total cost of the clusters
of S0 − Q is O(ε · opt). Following this procedure, we only have to remove inexpensive clusters.
While here it is not possible to identify the correct ones, making a mistake is not too costly. For any
c ∈ S0 − Q, we can now think of its whole cluster as being assigned entirely to a unique center in
S − S0 ∪ OPTˆ since by triangle inequality this would only increase its cost by O(ε · opt) in total. We
can thus think of the remaining centers S0 − Q as either being assigned to OPT     ˆ (we call this set R),
or to a center in S − S0 (we call this set U ). Suppose we know how many centers fall into these two
types, a figure we determine by trying all possibilities. The next step would be ideally to remove
U . We instead remove a proxy Ue of U , |Ue | = |U |(see Section 4.5). A natural starting point to find
a center for reassignment is to do so by greedily removing the center c for which the reassignment
cost is minimized. However, we must be careful. First, the reassignment costs are dynamic, that
is when removing a center, it affects reassignment costs of subsequently removed ones. Second, if a
center c was used to bound the reassigment cost of an deleted center, we cannot delete it later in order
to prevent the error from adding up. We design a procedure that produces a Ue satisfying all these
constraints (see Lemma 4.13). We remark that Ue ∩ R = ∅, hence removing R is still a valid option in
the later stages.
     Thus, for the purpose of analysis (the algorithm does not know R), we obtain the solutions MD     ′ =
                                ′                             ˆ
S − (Q ∪ U ∪ R) ∪ Λ and MO = S − (Q ∪ U ∪ R) ∪ OPT. Moreover, the costs satisfy cost( MO ) +
            e                                    e                                                   ′
cost( MD′ ) ≤ (4 + O ( ε ))OPT, which is the same as summed up costs of M and M , see Lemma 4.12
                                                                              O         D
and Claim 7.

Improvement via Submodular Maximization. We can now use submodular maximization tech-
niques (specifically algorithms to maximize a submodular function over a partition matroid con-
straint) in a similar but more general setting as in the work of [CGK+ 19]. In more detail, we wish to
find the best |OPTˆ | centers X to open (instead of the dummy centers Λ), one at the prescribed distance
from each leader, to improve the cost of MD   ′ as much as possible. This is almost identical to the work
          +
of [CGK 19] except that we also need to close |R| other centers R    e in S − Q − Ue. Ideally, we would
                ˆ
like to open OPT and close R. We show that selecting a good enough pair ( X, R)   e can be reduced to a
submodular maximization problem under a partition matroid constraint. In particular, we carefully
define our submodular function so that its value, for a given subset X of centers, is calculated by
optimally selecting the set R e to close (for the choice X). Then we can use state-of-the-art algorithms
to optimize this and simultaneously approximate the centers OPT  ˆ to open and the set R to close.


3    (2 + ε)-Approximation with O(log n/ε2 ) Extra Centers
In this section, we present a polynomial-time algorithm that opens k + O(log n/ε2 ) many centers and
returns a solution of cost at most (2 + ε)opt. We restate the theorem for convenience of the reader.
Theorem 1.2. For every constant ε ∈ (0, 1/6), there is a polynomial-time algorithm for k-median that returns
a solution containing at most k + O(log n/ε2 ) many centers and of cost at most (2 + ε)opt.
   Our algorithm is a non-trivial extension of the Greedy algorithm with dual fitting analysis [JMS02,
JMM+ 03], next called G REEDY, so as to guarantee that we only open k + O(log n/ε2 ) centers instead
of only doing so in expectation. We first recall the basics of G REEDY in Section 3.1. Section 3.2


                                                    12
presents a modified algorithm L OG A DAPTIVE that runs in O(log n/ε2 ) adaptive steps (that we call
phases) and Section 3.3 analyzes the latter algorithm.
    Finally, building on additional setups in Section 3.4, Section 3.5 presents our final algorithm
M ERGE S OLUTIONS achieving the guarantee of Theorem 1.2. It is an extension of L OG A DAPTIVE
maintaining two solutions that open at least and at most k centers respectively, while opening at most
three free centers per phase; this replaces the bipoint rounding [JV01, LS16, BPR+ 17, GPST23] and di-
rectly returns a (2 + ε) approximation that opens at most k + O(log n/ε2 ) centers. Its analysis, which
is an extension of the analysis of Section 3.3, is presented in Appendix A.

3.1     Review of the Greedy Algorithm
In this section, we provide a brief overview of the classic 2-approximate LMP algorithm from [JMS02,
JMM+ 03]. Our algorithms are derived by modifying this procedure, and these modifications become
more intuitive when we revisit the basic concepts of the original algorithm. We present it in Algo-
rithm 2 as a primal-dual algorithm to emphasize the changes we introduce.

  Algorithm 2 (G REEDY).
  Initialization: Set fˆ = 2 f , S = ∅, A = D and α j = 0 for every j ∈ A = D.

  While A ̸= ∅:

  Increase α-values of active clients (α j ) j∈ A uniformly until one of the following events happen:

       1. There is an unopened facility i whose bids pay for it:

                                  ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ = fˆ .
                                  j∈ A                     j∈ D − A

          Open i (i.e., add it to S) and mark all clients j ∈ A such that α j ≥ d( j, S) as served (i.e.,
          remove them from A).
       2. There is a client j ∈ A so that
                                                          α j = d( j, S).
          The client j is removed from the set A of active clients.



     The algorithm maintains the set A of active clients and the set S of opened facilities. Initially,
all clients are active, i.e., A = D, and no facility is open, i.e., S = ∅. Additionally, the algorithm
maintains an approximately feasible dual solution α, where each client j ∈ D has a corresponding
value α j . These values are initialized to 0.
     While there is at least one active client, the algorithm uniformly increases the α-values of the
active clients until there is a facility i whose ”bids” are sufficient to open it or an active client “gets
connected” to an already opened facility. The bids from a client j to a facility i depend on whether j
is active or not. If j ∈ A, then a portion of its budget α j , specifically d(i, j), is allocated to cover the
connection cost of j, and the remainder, α j − d(i, j), is used to bid towards the facility’s opening cost
fˆ := 2 f . For a client j ∈
                           / A, which is already connected to an open facility at a distance d( j, S), the bid
towards i is the difference in distance, i.e., d( j, S) − d(i, j) (or 0 if i is farther away from j than S).
     Finally, the algorithm marks any client j with α j ≥ d( j, S) as inactive, ensuring that at any point in
time, active clients j satisfy α j ≤ d( j, S).
     An overview of the analysis of G REEDY is as follows. We consider the final α-values denoted by
(α∗j ) j∈ A , and the final set S∗ of opened facilities. The definition of the bids is to guarantee that the
α∗ -values pay for both the opening costs of the facilities in S∗ and the connection costs of the clients:
Lemma 3.1. ∑ j∈ D α∗j = ∑ j∈ D d( j, S∗ ) + fˆ · |S∗ |.

      This is complemented by showing that α∗ /2 is a feasible dual solution.


                                                            13
Lemma 3.2. For a facility i, ∑ j∈ D [α∗j − 2d(i, j)]+ ≤ fˆ.

      To prove dual-feasibility, two key properties are used:
No overbidding: At any point in time, for every i ∈ F,

                                   ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ .
                                   j∈ A                   j∈ D − A


No active clients bid on open facilities: At any point in time, α j ≤ d( j, S) for j ∈ A.
Specifically, if we give a total order of the clients so that j ≤ ℓ if α∗j ≤ α∗ℓ , the above two properties
are used to prove the following technical lemma; intuitively, at time right before α∗ℓ , client j ∈ D − A
satisfies d( j, S) + d( j, i ) + d(i, ℓ) ≥ α∗ℓ , which implies d( j, S) − d( j, i ) ≥ α∗ℓ − 2d(i, j) − d(i, ℓ).
Lemma 3.3. For any ℓ ∈ D,

                          ∑ [α∗ℓ − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [α∗ℓ − 2d(i, j) − d(i, ℓ)]+ ≤ fˆ.
                        j∈ D:j≥ℓ                     j∈ D:j<ℓ



   Lemma 3.2 then follows by summing up the above inequality for all ℓ ∈ D. Let optLP ( f ) be the
value of an optimal solution to the linear programming relaxation when opening costs are f . Then,
we get the following theorem by combining Lemma 3.1 and Lemma 3.2.
Theorem 3.4. The set S∗ of facilities output by G REEDY satisfies

                                      ∑ d( j, S∗ ) ≤ 2 · (optLP ( f ) − f · |S∗ |) .
                                      j∈ D



    Recall that optLP ( f ) − f · k is a lower bound on the optimal solution to the k-median problem (see
Section 1.3). Hence, if G REEDY outputs a solution S∗ such that |S∗ | = k, then it is a 2-approximate
solution to k-median. Moreover, we can always set f high enough so that any good solution opens at
most k facilities and f low enough so that any good solution opens at least k facilities. Thus, a simple
binary search can find values f and f ′ such that running G REEDY with opening costs f opens k1 ≥ k
facilities, while running it with opening costs f ′ opens k2 ≤ k facilities. However, even though we
can ensure that | f − f ′ | is exponentially small, say 1/2n , it turns out that G REEDY is quite unstable,
and we may have k1 ≪ k and k2 ≫ k, which does not directly imply anything useful for k-median.
In other works, this has been addressed through an additional rounding algorithm called bi-point
rounding, which introduces an extra factor into the approximation guarantee. In contrast, we modify
G REEDY into a similar algorithm that is “smoother” and less adaptive in its selection of facilities to
open.

3.2     L OG A DAPTIVE
It is natural to think of the G REEDY as increasing the α-values of active clients with a time θ. That is,
we always have α j = θ for j ∈ A. A difficulty in ensuring that G REEDY opens k facilities is that it
is a very adaptive algorithm. To see this, consider the case when two facilities i and i′ both become
paid for at the same time θ. Whether we open i or i′ can dramatically change the bids to future
facilities and completely change the trace and what facilities are opened by the algorithm. Our idea
to overcome this difficulty is to limit the number of time steps where such decisions are made, thus
lowering the adaptivity of the algorithm. Specifically, our high-level goal is to only consider the time
steps of the form (1 + ε2 )0 , (1 + ε2 )1 , (1 + ε2 )2 , . . .. Then, as the minimum nonzero distance is 1 and
the maximum distance is poly(n), the standard arguments (formalized in Section 3.5) imply that the
total number of time steps that we need to consider is O(log(n)/ε2 ), and this is what we refer to as
O(log n) adaptivity.
     The difficulty in only considering those time steps is that we easily lose the property that we
never overbid a facility i, which is crucial in the analysis of G REEDY. Indeed, consider a time step


                                                           14
θ = (1 + ε2 )t and let A and I be the current active and inactive clients, respectively. Now, suppose
that there is a facility i whose bids

                                          ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+
                                          j∈ A                     j∈ I

are just barely below fˆ. However, in that case, increasing the α-value of active clients by a factor 1 + ε2
may cause the bids to be strictly above fˆ, leading to difficulties in the analysis. (Specifically, in the
proof that α∗ /2 is dual feasible.) We intuitively change G REEDY in two ways to handle this. First,
we open centers if the bids pay for them in the metric scaled down by a factor of (1 − δ). That is, we
consider a facility i paid for if

                         ∑ [α j − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ [d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≥ fˆ .
                        j∈ A                                                j∈ I

As we scale down the metric (i.e., the connection cost) by a factor 1 − δ, this loses a factor 1/(1 − δ)
in the approximation guarantee. However, the benefit is the following. Let X = { j ∈ A | α j ≥
(1 − δ)d( j, i )} be the active clients that bid for i. Then
                                                                               
                              ∑ α j − (1 − δ)d(i, j) ≥ ∑ (1 + ε2 )α j − d(i, j)
                                                      
                                j∈ X                                 j∈ X

assuming that ∑ j∈X ε2 α j = ε2 θ | X | is smaller than δ ∑ j∈X d(i, j). We will select δ = 3ε and so this
means that it is safe to increase the α-values of active clients by a factor (1 + ε2 ) as long as the average
distance from bidding active clients is at least ε2 θ/δ = εθ/3. Our second change deals with the
remaining case when the clients in X are close to co-located with i. We do so by allowing the clients
j in the ball B(i, εθ ) to increase their bids for i up to min((1 + ε2 )θ, (1 − δ)d( j, S)). These changes are
captured in the following definition of openable facilities. The bids (τj ) j∈ A from active clients are
only allowed to increase for nearby clients (the first two properties). A facility is considered paid for
with respect to the scaled down metric (the third property). Finally, for the proof of dual feasibility
even after increasing the bids of nearby clients, we need a technical fourth condition in the definition.
We remark that we use (α, S, A, θ ) to denote the algorithm’s state, where α is the approximate feasible
dual solution, S the set of open facilities, A the set of active clients (which determines I = D \ A), and
θ the time. Both G REEDY and L OG A DAPTIVE (that we describe below) satisfy α j = θ for all j ∈ A.
Definition 2. A facility i is openable with respect to the algorithm’s state (α, S, A, θ ) if there are bids (τj ) j∈ A
of active clients that satisfy the following conditions.
   • Only nearby clients are increased:
                                                 τj = α j   for every j ∈ A − B(i, εθ ).

   • Nearby clients are only increased slightly:

                      α j ≤ τj ≤ min{(1 − δ)d( j, S), (1 + ε2 )θ }                        for every j ∈ A ∩ B(i, εθ ).

   • Facility i is paid for:

                               ∑ [τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ [d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+ ≥ fˆ .
                            j∈ A                                                   j∈ I

   • Dual feasibility: for every facility i0 and ℓ ∈ A,

                                   ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [τℓ − 2d( j, i0 ) − d(ℓ, i0 )]+ ≤ fˆ.
                                   j∈ A                     j∈ I


    We note that when the state of the algorithm is clear from the context, we simply say that ”i is
openable” without explicitly specifying the state.
    The following lemma follows from that we can write the selection of the bids as a linear program.
Indeed, given the state (α, S, A, θ ) of the algorithm, each of the four conditions on the bids (τj ) j∈ A can
be formulated as linear inequalities.


                                                               15
Lemma 3.5. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given the state (α, S, A, θ ) and a facility i, either
outputs bids (τ ) j∈ A that satisfy all the conditions of Definition 2 or certifies that i is not openable.
Proof. Fix i and (α, S, A, θ ) and consider a linear program with variables (τj ) j∈ A . We claim that each
of the four bullets in Definition 2 can be expressed as linear constraints. Indeed, the first two bullets
are immediate. As τj = α j for every j ∈ A − B(i, εθ ), we can treat them as constants and only have
(τj ) j∈ A∩ B(i,εθ ) as the true variables. Then, the third bullet,

                         ∑ [τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ [d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+ ≥ fˆ
                         j∈ A                                     j∈ I

becomes a linear inequality in the true variables, since the only terms involving the true variables
are [τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ for j ∈ A ∩ B(i, εθ ), and for those j, [τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ is indeed equal to
(τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)) without [·]+ because (τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)) ≥ θ − (1 − δ)εθ > 0.
    Finally, the fourth bullet

                                ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [τℓ − 2d( j, i0 ) − d(ℓ, i0 )]+ ≤ fˆ
                                j∈ A                  j∈ I

is equivalent to introducing variables t j for j ∈ D and adding the following constraints: (1) t j ≥
τj − d(i0 , j) for j ∈ A, (2) t j ≥ τℓ − 2d( j, i0 ) − d(ℓ, i0 ) for j ∈ I, (3) t j ≥ 0 for j ∈ D, and (4) ∑ j∈ A t j +
∑ j∈ I t j ≤ fˆ.

    The description of L OG A DAPTIVE is given in Algorithm 3. It takes a parameter ε ∈ [0, 1/6] and

  Algorithm 3 (L OG A DAPTIVE).
  Initialization: Set fˆ = 2 f , S = ∅, A = D, θ = 1 and α j = θ for every j ∈ A = D.

  Repeat the following while A ̸= ∅:

      1. While there is an unopened facility i that is openable (chosen arbitrarily if there are many),
               Calculate (τj ) j∈ A so that i is openable with bids (τj ) j∈ A . Open i (i.e., add it to S),
               let α j ← τj for every j ∈ A (note that only the values of the clients in A ∩ B(i, εθ )
               change), mark all clients j ∈ A such that α j ≥ (1 − δ)d( j, S) as served (i.e., remove
               them from A).
      2. Perform the following step and proceed to the next phase.

               For every remaining j ∈ A, let α j ← min((1 + ε2 )θ, (1 − δ)d( j, S)), remove it from
               A when α j = (1 − δ)d( j, S). Update θ = (1 + ε2 )θ.




δ = 3ε. The time θ is now increased in steps by a multiplicative factor of (1 + ε2 ). This is done in each
iteration of the outermost loop, ”Repeat the following while A ̸= ∅,” which we refer to as a phase
of the algorithm. Inside a phase, we refer to the two points as stages, so stage 1 corresponds to the
while-loop where we open openable facilities, and stage 2 corresponds to the increase of α-values of
the active clients A. At any point in time, the state of the algorithm is described, as aforementioned,
by the tuple (α, S, A, θ ). The careful reader may observe that A is redundant because the active clients
A are determined by S. Indeed, the same holds for the set of inactive clients I = D − A = { j ∈ D :
α j < (1 − δ)d( j, S)}. However, it will be notationally convenient to explicitly keep track of A in the
algorithm’s state.
    The parameter ε > 0 regulates the number of phases the algorithm will make. Indeed, all active
clients have the same α-value, and after the completion of p phases, the α-value of active clients equals
(1 + ε2 ) p . This ensures that the number of phases until the algorithm terminates is O(log(n)/ε2 ).
Hence, the increase of α-values only depends on O(log(n)/ε2 ) states of the algorithm, the states


                                                             16
before the second stage in each of the phases. This is why we refer to the algorithm to be of logarithmic
adaptivity.
   Apart from impacting the number of phases, the choice of ε also impacts the approximation guar-
antee. Specifically, we prove the following theorem in the subsequent section. (Recall that δ = 3ε.)


Theorem 3.6. The set S∗ of facilities output by L OG A DAPTIVE satisfies

                                                           2
                                 ∑ d( j, S∗ ) ≤ 1 − δ · (optLP ( f ) − f · |S∗ |) .
                                 j∈ D




3.3     Analysis of L OG A DAPTIVE
An atomic step refers to the set of operations in the indented paragraphs in L OG A DAPTIVE that change
the state (α, S, A, θ ). (I.e., an atomic step happens when a new facility i is opened and at the end of
each phase.) By “at any point of the algorithm”, we mean any point in the algorithm’s execution that
is not in the middle of an atomic step. Throughout the analysis, we let α∗j be the final α-value of a
client j ∈ D, and we let S∗ be the set of opened facilities. We start by analyzing the approximation
guarantee of the algorithm with respect to α∗ , and we then prove that α∗ /2 is a feasible solution to
the dual.

3.3.1   Approximation Guarantee.
In the following lemma, we prove that ∑ j∈ D α∗j pays for the opening costs of the facilities in S∗ and
the connection cost of the clients scaled by a factor (1 − δ).

Lemma 3.7. We have ∑ j∈ D α∗j ≥ (1 − δ) ∑ j∈ D d( j, S∗ ) + fˆ |S∗ |.

    In the next subsection, we prove the following lemma showing that α∗ /2 is a feasible dual solu-
tion.
Lemma 3.8. For every facility i, ∑ j∈ D [α∗j − 2d(i, j)]+ ≤ fˆ.

   These two lemmas imply

                                                    α∗j        1−δ
                              optLP ( f ) ≥ ∑
                                                                2 j∑
                                                           ≥          d( j, S∗ ) + f |S∗ |,
                                                j∈ D
                                                     2             ∈D

where we used that fˆ = 2 · f . Therefore, we have an LMP 2/(1 − δ)-approximation: the connection
cost of the output solution S∗ satisfies

                                                           2
                                  ∑ d( j, S∗ ) ≤ 1 − δ (optLP ( f ) − f |S∗ |) .
                                  j∈ D

In other words, Lemma 3.7 and Lemma 3.8 imply Theorem 3.6.
    We proceed to prove Lemma 3.7 (and prove Lemma 3.8 in the next subsection).

Proof of Lemma 3.7. We prove by induction that at any point of the algorithm

                                         ∑ α j ≥ ∑ (1 − δ)d( j, S) + fˆ |S|.                          (3)
                                         j∈ I       j∈ I

The equality is initially true since A = D (thus I = ∅) and S = ∅. We now analyze each of the two
stages separately.




                                                               17
Stage 1. Consider what happens when we open a facility i, i.e., add it to S. Let (α, S, A, θ ) be the
state right before opening i, A′ be the clients that are removed from A by the opening, which means
A′ = { j ∈ A : τj ≥ (1 − δ)d(i, j)}. (Recall that the opening lets α j ← τj for j ∈ A.) Further, let
X := { j ∈ I : d( j, i ) < d( j, S)} be the subset of clients of I that make positive bids to i. The change of
cost of the right-hand side of (3) is at most

                            fˆ + ∑ (1 − δ)d(i, j) + ∑ (1 − δ)(d(i, j) − d( j, S)) .
                                  j∈ A′                 j∈ X

Since i is fully paid (the third bullet of Definition 2), we also have

                        fˆ ≤ ∑ (τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)) + ∑ (1 − δ)(d( j, S) − d(i, j)) .
                             j∈ A′                             j∈ X

We thus get that the change of cost of the right-hand side is at most ∑ j∈ A′ τj , which is the change of
the left-hand side.

Stage 2. No facility is open at this stage, and every client j that becomes inactive at this stage has
α j = (1 − δ)d( j, S), which implies that the left-hand side and right-hand side both increase with the
same amount when a client j becomes inactive.

3.3.2   Dual Feasibility
We start by showing that we never overbid a facility i. This is rather immediate in the analysis
of G REEDY that we outlined in Section 3.1. While we prove this remains true for L OG A DAPTIVE,
proving it requires more work, especially because of the discrete increase of α-values in the second
stage of a phase.
Claim 1 (No Over-Bidding). At any point of the algorithm, for every facility i,

                            ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ.                        (4)
                           j∈ A                  j∈ I



Proof. In the beginning, the claim is true because every client is active and α j = 1 ≤ d(i, j) for every
j ∈ D. Let us prove that each step of the algorithm maintains this invariant. We first consider the
steps that happen during the first stage and then those during the second stage.

Stage 1. Suppose that facility i′ is open with the bids (τj ) j∈ A . It does (1) potentially increase the α-
values of clients in B(i′ , εθ ) ∩ A, and (2) make clients in A ∩ B(i′ , θ/(1 − δ)) inactive, which includes
B(i′ , εθ ) ∩ A considered in (1).
     Consider any facility i and see how (4) is impacted by this change. First, observe that a client j that
was inactive before the opening of i′ does not increase its contribution to (4). This is because d( j, S)
monotonically decreases in S. We now turn our attention to the more interesting case. For a client j
becoming inactive from active, the contribution to (4) changes from [α j − d(i, j)]+ (where α-values are
measured right before the opening) to [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i )]+ (where S includes i′ ).
     If α j does not change by the opening of i′ , the fact that j becomes inactive means (1 − δ)d( j, S) ≤ α j ,
so j’s contribution to (4) cannot increase. If α j does strictly increase by the opening of i′ , it means that
d( j, S) ≤ d( j, i′ ) ≤ εθ after the opening, so the contribution to (4) decreases by at least θ − (1 − δ)εθ or
becomes 0.
     We have thus proved that no client increases their contribution to (4) during the steps of the first
stage, and so these steps maintain the inequality.

Stage 2. Assume towards contradiction that at the end of the phase, if we increase α j of every j ∈ A
to min((1 + ε2 )θ, (1 − δ)d( j, S)), the claim is violated for a non-empty subset of facilities F ′ . We select




                                                         18
a “minimal” such counter example in the following way: let τ ′ ≤ (1 + ε2 )θ be the smallest value so
that α′j := min(τ ′ , (1 − δ)d( j, S)) satisfies

                                      ∑ [α′j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ = fˆ,                                   (5)
                                      j∈ A                       j∈ I

for some i ∈ F ′ . We remark that τ ′ ≥ θ since (4) is satisfied for all facilities after the first stage by the
previous arguments. Furthermore, i ̸∈ S is not yet open since if it was open then there would be no
client j ∈ A for which α′j − d(i, j) is strictly positive. Hence the increase in α-values cannot cause i to
violate (4) if it were already opened, which would contradict i ∈ F ′ .
    We now show that this i with τj = α′j for j ∈ A ∩ B(i, εθ ) and τj = α j for j ∈ A − B(i, εθ ) satisfies
the conditions of Definition 2 for i. In other words, i is an openable facility (that was not yet opened),
which contradicts the completion of the first stage. We verify the conditions of Definition 2 one-by-
one. The first two bullets are satisfied by the definition of the bids (τj ) j∈ A . For the third bullet,

       ∑ [τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+

 =            ∑         [α′j − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ +               ∑          [α j − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ [d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+

To upper bound the second summation we used that α j − (1 − δ)d(i, j) ≥ (1 + ε2 )α j − d(i, j) ≥ α′j −
                               ε2 α
d(i, j) when d(i, j) ≥ δ j = εθ3 (recall that α j = θ for all j ∈ A), condition which is satisfied by the
clients not in B(i, εθ ).
    It remains to verify the fourth bullet of Definition 2. Suppose toward contradiction that there is a
facility i0 and ℓ ∈ A such that

                                  ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [τℓ − d(i0 , ℓ) − 2d(i0 , j)]+ > fˆ.
                                  j∈ A                           j∈ I

Note that ℓ ∈ A implies τℓ ≤ (1 − δ)d(ℓ, S) and for every j ∈ I, (1 − δ)d(ℓ, S) ≤ (1 − δ)(d(ℓ, i0 ) +
d(i0 , j) + d( j, S)). Thus

                                       τℓ − d(i0 , ℓ) − 2d(i0 , j) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i0 , j) .                                 (6)

We conclude that

                                       ∑ [α′j − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i0 , j)]+
                                       j∈ A                         j∈ I
