                                                           +
                                       j∈ A                         j∈ I
                                (6)
                                ≥ ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [τℓ − d(i0 , ℓ) − 2d(i0 , j)]+ > fˆ
                                       j∈ A                         j∈ I

This implies that we could have further lowered τ ′ while keeping equality (5) true for i0 . This con-
tradicts the minimality of τ ′ .


   Having proved that L OG A DAPTIVE as G REEDY maintains the invariant that there is no overbid-
ding, we are ready to prove the dual-feasibility of α∗ /2. (This corresponds to Lemma 3.2 in the
analysis of G REEDY.)

Lemma 3.8. For every facility i, ∑ j∈ D [α∗j − 2d(i, j)]+ ≤ fˆ.




                                                                           19
    We remark that this implies dual feasibility of α∗ /2 by dividing both sides by 2 (recall fˆ = 2 · f ).
    The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of Lemma 3.8, and from now on, we focus on an
arbitrary but fixed facility i ∈ F. Let D ∗ = { j ∈ D : α∗j > 2d(i, j)} be those clients that contribute
to the left-hand side of the inequality in the claim. Further, order the clients in D ∗ according to α∗
values by the time they are removed from A (in the algorithm) and break ties according to α∗ values
(in increasing order). We will use j, ℓ to denote clients in D ∗ , and let us slightly abuse notation and
use j ≤ ℓ with respect to this ordering. In other words, we have j ≤ ℓ if j was removed from A
in an atomic step before ℓ was removed, or j and ℓ were removed during the same atomic step and
α∗j ≤ α∗ℓ . We also use the terminology that a facility i0 freezes i if i0 becomes open when i is not, and
d(i, i0 ) ≤ (1 + 3ε)θ/2. (Of course, i freezes itself when it is open.) This notion is important as for a
facility i, its D ∗ and their α∗ values do not change after it is frozen.
    The following lemma, analogous to Lemma 3.3 in the analysis of G REEDY, is key to proving dual
feasibility.
Lemma 3.9. For any ℓ ∈ D ∗ that becomes inactive strictly before i becomes frozen,

                         ∑
                         ∗
                                  [α∗ℓ − d(i, j)]+ +          ∑
                                                              ∗
                                                                   [α∗ℓ − 2d(i, j) − d(i, ℓ)]+ ≤ fˆ.           (7)
                      j∈ D :j≥ℓ                        j∈ D :j<ℓ



Proof. Let us do the following case analysis depending on the stage ℓ becomes inactive.

Stage 1. ℓ becomes inactive because of the opening of some i0 . By the statement of the lemma, it
suffices to handle the case that i0 does not freeze i. So d(i, i0 ) > (1 + 3ε)θ/2. The opening of i0 might
have strictly increased the α-value of the clients in B(i0 , εθ ), but no such client j will be in D ∗ , since
they immediately become inactive while

                  d(i, j) ≥ d(i, i0 ) − d(i0 , j) > ((1 + 3ε)/2 − ε)θ ≥ (1 + ε2 )θ/2 ≥ α∗j /2.

Applying this argument to every facility that is open in this phase (which did not freeze i), we can
conclude that, right before i0 is open, every j ∈ D ∗ has α j ≤ θ and all active ones have α j = θ. (So,
α∗ℓ = θ as well since the opening of i0 does not strictly increase αℓ .) Claim 1, applied right before i0 is
open, ensures that
                           ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+ ≤ fˆ.
                           j∈ A                        j∈ I

This satisfies (7) as (1) every j ∈ D ∗ with j ≥ ℓ is in j ∈ A at that point by our ordering of the clients
within D ∗ , and (2) for every j < ℓ with j ∈ I,

                          α∗ℓ < (1 − δ)d(ℓ, S) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, S) + d( j, i ) + d(i, ℓ)

implies
                              α∗ℓ − 2d(i, j) − d(i, ℓ) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i, j).
(Note that this S does not contain i0 and ℓ was not connected yet.)

Stage 2. Suppose that ℓ becomes inactive by the increase of the α-values at the end of a phase. Then
Claim 1 ensures that, at the end of this phase (after ℓ becomes inactive),

                           ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ.                          (8)
                           j∈ A                        j∈ I

    Let us see that the left-hand side of (8) is at least that of (7) by comparing the contribution of j ∈ D ∗
to both. If j ∈ A, its contribution to the former is definitely at least that to the latter, since any active j
has α j = (1 + ε2 )θ ≥ α∗ℓ .
    For j ∈ I, we must have

      α∗ℓ ≤ (1 − δ)d(ℓ, S) ≤ (1 − δ)(d(ℓ, i ) + d(i, j) + d( j, S)) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, S) + d(ℓ, i ) + d(i, j) .


                                                              20
(since αℓ is never increased above (1 − δ)d(ℓ, S)). Thus

                                  α∗ℓ − 2d(i, j) − d(i, ℓ) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i, j)

which satisfies our goal when j < ℓ.
    Finally, when j ∈ I but j ≥ ℓ, it means that j was removed at the same time as ℓ at the end of the
phase. In that case (1 − δ)d( j, S) = α∗j and, as we break ties with α∗ -values, α∗j ≥ α∗ℓ , which again
satisfies our goal.
   Equipped with the above lemma, we are ready to complete the proof of dual feasibility.
Proof of Lemma 3.8. Consider the case that i is frozen by i0 with (τj ) j∈ A . We first prove the following
claim that says that after i is frozen the α-values of clients in D ∗ are finalized.
Claim. We have α∗j = τj for every j ∈ D ∗ ∩ A.

Proof of Claim. Consider j ∈ A. First, note that if τj ≥ (1 − δ)d( j, i0 ) then j is removed from A when i0
is opened and so α∗j = τj .
    In the other case, when α j ≤ τj < (1 − δ)d( j, i0 ) we show that j ̸∈ D ∗ . Consider the (future) time
right after j is removed from the active clients. The α-value of j then (which is equal to the final α∗j )
cannot be strictly greater than (1 − δ)d( j, i0 ); whether it is increased in stage 1 (as α ← τ) or stage
2, since i0 ∈ S (where S is the set of open facilities right before j becomes inactive), the algorithm
ensures that it cannot be strictly more than (1 − δ)d( j, S) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, i0 ). By the triangle inequality
                                                                                  
                                                                 d ( i0 , i ) θ
                       d( j, i ) ≥ d( j, i0 ) − d(i0 , i ) = 1 −                     d( j, i0 ) ,
                                                                      θ d( j, i0 )

where θ is considered at the time when i0 was open. As i0 freezes i, we have d(i0 , i ) ≤ (1 + 3ε)θ/2
and, by the assumption of the case we have θ = α j ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, i0 ). Plugging in those bounds to the
above inequality yields,
                                                              
                                               (1 + 3ε)
                               d( j, i ) ≥ 1 −          (1 − δ) d( j, i0 ) .
                                                  2

We rewrite the above inequality (multiplying both sides by two and using that δ = 3ε) to obtain
                                                                          
                    2d( j, i ) ≥ (2 − (1 + 3ε)(1 − δ)) d( j, i0 ) = 1 + 9ε2 d( j, i0 ) .

In other words we have α∗j ≤ d( j, i0 ) < 2d( j, i ), so j cannot be in D ∗ .

    Having proved the above claim, we proceed to analyze the dual feasibility. As the α-values of
clients in A ∩ D ∗ remain unchanged after the removal of i0 , the fourth bullet of Definition 2 ensures
that, for any ℓ ∈ A ∩ D ∗ ,

                          ∑ ∗ [α∗j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ ∗ [α∗ℓ − 2d(i, j) − d(ℓ, i)]+ ≤ fˆ.                      (9)
                        j∈ A∩ D                         j∈ I ∩ D

   Moreover, for every client ℓ ∈ D ∗ ∩ I that became inactive strictly before i0 was opened, Lemma 3.9
goves

                          ∑
                          ∗
                                   [α∗ℓ − d(i, j)]+ +      ∑
                                                           ∗
                                                                    [α∗ℓ − 2d(i, j) − d(i, ℓ)]+ ≤ fˆ.     (10)
                       j∈ D :j≥ℓ                        j∈ D :j<ℓ

Let us consider the summation of (10) for every ℓ ∈ I ∩ D ∗ and (9) for every ℓ ∈ A ∩ D ∗ , and consider
how many times each term appears. Let a = | A ∩ D ∗ |, b = | D ∗ |.

   • −d(i, j′ ) when j′ ∈ I ∩ D ∗ : Say j′ is the qth client in D ∗ for some q ≤ b − a. Then −d(i, j′ ) appears
     b + (b − q) times in (9) (2a times) and (10) (2(b − a − q) + q times) as −d(i, j) and (q − 1) times
     as −d(i, ℓ) in (10) when ℓ = j′ , so the total is 2b − 1.


                                                              21
   • −d(i, j′ ) when j′ ∈ A ∩ D ∗ : It appears b times in (9) and (10) as −d(i, j) and b − a times as
     −d(i, ℓ) in (10) when ℓ = j′ , so the total is 2b − a.
   • α∗j′ for j′ ∈ I ∩ D ∗ : It appears b times in (10) as α∗ℓ when ℓ = j′ .

   • α∗j′ for j′ ∈ A ∩ D ∗ : It appears b − a times in (9) as α∗ℓ when ℓ = j′ and once in (9) as α∗j for each
     ℓ ∈ A ∩ D ∗ , so b times in total.

Summarizing we have,

                      b ∑ α∗j − (2b − a)        ∑         d(i, j) − (2b − 1)     ∑          d(i, j) ≤ b fˆ,
                        j∈ D ∗               j∈ D ∗ ∩ A                        j∈ D ∗ ∩ I

and so in particular ∑ j∈ D∗ α∗j − 2d(i, j) ≤ fˆ, which proves the lemma in the case the opening of a
facility i0 freezes i.
    To complete the proof, when no facility freezes i, Lemma 3.9 holds for every client in D ∗ , so we can
simply sum up (10) over all clients in D ∗ , which corresponds to the special case of the above analysis
when D ∗ ∩ A = ∅.

3.4    Walking Between Two Solutions: Setup
Given the description of the L OG A DAPTIVE, we present our final algorithm M ERGE S OLUTIONS, re-
sulting in a solution that opens k + Oε (log n) centers by maintaining two partial solutions that lead
to opening at least k and at most k centers respectively and gradually merge them into a single solution
that opens exactly k + Oε (log n) facilities, exploiting the Oε (log n) adaptivity of L OG A DAPTIVE.
    Before detailing the merging procedure, which we call M ERGE S OLUTIONS, it is helpful to observe
that we may generalize the analysis to make it more “robust”. We introduce this more robust analysis
in this subsection, where we also define the notation that will be used later to describe M ERGE S OLU -
TIONS in Section 3.5.
    Starting from the two solutions obtained in the standard way whose facility costs differ by at
most η := 2−n , the two solutions we maintain will have an exponentially small difference η in one
of their parameters. (These parameters will be either the facility cost f or some distance related to a
free facility that will be introduced later.) For this reason, when we merge the two solutions, it may
occur that some facilities in one solution are almost completely paid for, rather than fully paid for. To
address this, we generalize the definition of ”openable” to ”η-openable,” where the only difference is
in the third condition: we now require each facility that is opened to be paid fˆ − ηn instead of fˆ (as
in Definition 2). We also note that being openable only depends on the time θ and the set S of open
facilities so far. This is because α j = θ for j ∈ A, I = { j ∈ D : θ ≥ (1 − δ)d( j, S)} and A = D − I. This
allows us to simplify notation and we have the following generalization of Definition 2.
Definition 3. Consider the time θ and let S be the set of opened facilities. Then set of inactive and active clients
are I = { j ∈ D : θ ≥ (1 − δ)d( j, S)} and A = D − I, respectively. We say that a facility i ∈ F is η-openable
(with respect to θ and S) if there are bids (τj ) j∈ A of active clients that satisfy the following conditions.
   • Only nearby clients are increased:

                                        τj = α j          for every j ∈ A − B(i, εθ ).

   • Nearby clients are only increased slightly:

                      α j ≤ τj ≤ min{(1 − δ)d( j, S), (1 + ε2 )θ }             for every j ∈ A ∩ B(i, εθ ).

   • Facility i is paid for (up to the error parameter η):

                        ∑ [τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ [d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+ ≥ fˆ − nη .
                        j∈ A                                       j∈ I




                                                             22
   • Dual feasibility: for every facility i0 and ℓ ∈ A,

                                ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [τℓ − 2d( j, i0 ) − d(ℓ, i0 )]+ ≤ fˆ.
                               j∈ A                       j∈ I



    We also introduce the concept of free facilities. During the process of merging the two solutions,
we introduce free facilities, whose opening costs are not necessarily paid. While we will introduce at
most O(log n) such free facilities, we do not restrict the number of free copies a regular facility may
have. Let Sfree ⊆ S denote the multiset of free facilities that have been opened.
    Moreover, for each free copy ĩ ∈ Sfree of i ∈ F, we define a parameter u(ĩ ), which represents the
distance between the freely opened facility ĩ and its original copy i. So for any point x in the metric
space (either a facility or a client), the distance is given by d(ĩ, x ) = u(ĩ ) + d(i, x ).
    We further introduce the following terminology: as mentioned, we refer to the facilities in Sfree as
free facilities, the facilities in F and Sreg = S − Sfree as regular facilities, and when we simply mention
”facilities,” we are referring to their union.
    Finally, we will use the letters i and h to denote facilities. Specifically, we use i ∈ F to denote a
regular facility and use ĩ to denote a free copy of i. When referring to a facility that can be either free
or regular, we use the letter h.
    We now introduce the definition of valid sequences, which aims to capture the sequence of facili-
ties that are opened during the while-loop of stage 1 in L OG A DAPTIVE.
Definition 4 (η-valid sequence). Consider the time θ and let S be the set of opened facilities. A sequence
⟨h1 , . . . , hℓ ⟩ of facilities is η-valid (with respect to θ and S), if the following conditions hold.
   • Each ht is either free or η-openable with respect to the time θ and a superset S′ ⊇ S ∪ { h1 , . . . , ht−1 } of
     opened facilities.
   • The sequence is maximal, i.e., there is no openable facility with respect to the time θ and S ∪ { h1 , . . . , hℓ }.

    We remark that L OG A DAPTIVE generates 0-valid sequences in each phase. Specifically, it gener-
ates sequences H = ( H1 , H2 , . . . , HL ), where each H p is 0-valid (with respect to θ = (1 + ε2 ) p−1 and
S = ∪i∈[ p−1] Hi ) and contains only regular facilities. The set S of opened facilities is such that A = ∅
at the final time θ.
    For a general sequence of sequences H = ( H1 , H2 , . . . , HL ), let us say it consists of η-valid se-
quences if, for every p ∈ [ L], H p is an η-valid sequence with respect to θ = (1 + ε2 ) p−1 and S =
∪q∈[ p−1] Hq . (Here we abuse notation and treat Hq as a set of facilities.) Furthermore, we say that H
is a solution if the set S of opened facilities ensures that A = ∅ at the end, meaning that at the time
θ = (1 + ε2 ) L of the ( L + 1)st phase, the set of inactive clients I = { j | θ ≥ (1 − δ)d( j, S)} equals all
clients in D. Otherwise, we say that H is a partial solution.
    Theorem 3.6 can thus be stated as: if H is a solution of 0-valid sequences consisting of regular
facilities then the set S of opened facilities satisfies

                                                      2
                                      ∑ d( j, S) ≤ 1 − δ · (optLP ( f ) − f · |S|) .
                                      j∈ D


We now allow to open regular facilities by paying at least fˆ − ηn (instead of fˆ) and incorporate the no-
tion free facilities, which leads to the following generalization of the analysis, proved in Appendix A.


Theorem 3.10. Consider a solution H of η-valid sequences and let S be the opened facilities. Further, let Sfree
and Sreg = S − Sfree be the free copies and regular facilities, respectively. Then

                                                 2
                              ∑ d( j, S) ≤ 1 − δ · (optLP ( f ) − ( f − ηn) · |Sreg |) .
                             j∈ D




                                                             23
    It will be useful to use similar terminology as we did in Section 3.3, let us consider the execution of
the solution H where for p = 1, . . . , L, we run the p-th phase according to the sequence H p ; in Stage
1, each facility in the sequence becomes open one by one with the corresponding (τj ) j values, and in
Stage 2 at the end of the phase, θ ← θ (1 + ε2 ).
    Then an atomic step can still be defined in the same way as in L OG A DAPTIVE; it corresponds
to opening a facility in Stage 1 (and updating α, S, A accordingly) or increasing α values in Stage 2
simultaneously (and updating S accordingly). By “at any point of the algorithm”, we mean any point
in the algorithm’s execution that is not in the middle of an atomic step.
    With this notation, we have the following generalization of Claim 1:

      At any point of the algorithm, for every facility i,

                               ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ.              (11)
                               j∈ A                     j∈ I


This is formally proved in Claim 10 in Appendix A, and the proof is essentially the same as that
of Claim 1. We use it to prove Lemma 3.11, which we will use in the merging procedure to ensure
maximality. At a point of the execution, we say that a facility i is payable if it is openable (with η = 0)
except that the dual feasibility condition is not necessarily satisfied. The payable notion is thus a
weaker notion than openable, and a facility i may be payable but not openable. However, we have
the following lemma that says that if there is a payable facility, there is also an openable facility. We
remark that it does not guarantee that a payable facility is also openable but it does guarantee that if
there exists a payable facility, there is also an openable facility, which is sufficient for our arguments.
Lemma 3.11. At any point of the algorithm, if there is a payable facility, then there is also an openable facility
(that is not yet opened).
Proof. The argument is similar to that of the “Stage 2” proof of Claim 1. Suppose there is a non-empty
subset of facilities F ′ that are payable. To prove the lemma, we need to show that there is an openable
facility.
    We choose a “minimal” payable facility as follows. Let τ ′ ∈ [θ, (1 + ε2 )θ ] be the smallest value
such that there exists some i ∈ F ′ , for which the values τj , defined by
                                       (
                                           min{τ ′ , (1 − δ)d( j, S)}, if j ∈ A ∩ B(i, εθ ),
                               τj :=
                                           αj,                         otherwise,

satisfy
                        ∑ [τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ [d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≥ fˆ,                     (12)
                        j∈ A                                        j∈ I

for some i ∈ F ′ . We remark that i ̸∈ S is not yet open since if it was open then there would be no
client j ∈ A for which τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j) is strictly positive and the bids of inactive clients would
be 0. (Strictly speaking, here we assume fˆ > 0 since otherwise all facilities are opened at the very
beginning of the algorithm and there is nothing to prove.)
     We now show that this τ satisfies the conditions of Definition 3 for i. In other words, i is an
openable facility (that was not yet opened), which implies the lemma. We verify the conditions of
Definition 3 with η = 0 one-by-one. The first two bullets are satisfied by the definition of the bids
(τj ) j∈ A . The third bullet is satisfied by (12). Finally, we verify the dual feasibility condition. Suppose
toward contradiction that there is a facility i0 and ℓ ∈ A such that

                               ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [τℓ − d(i0 , ℓ) − 2d(i0 , j)]+ > fˆ.
                           j∈ A                         j∈ I

Note that ℓ ∈ A implies τℓ ≤ (1 − δ)d(ℓ, S) and for every j ∈ I, (1 − δ)d(ℓ, S) ≤ (1 − δ)(d(ℓ, i0 ) +

                                τℓ − d(i0 , ℓ) − 2d(i0 , j) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i0 , j) .                   (13)



                                                               24
We conclude that

               ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i0 , j)]+
              j∈ A                              j∈ I

                                            ≥ ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [τℓ − d(i0 , ℓ) − 2d(i0 , j)]+ > fˆ .
                                                   j∈ A                 j∈ I

We remark that this implies that τ ′ > θ since otherwise, as then τj = α j = θ for every j ∈ A, the above
inequality would contradict (11).
   We proceed to prove that the above inequality implies that we could have further lowered τ ′ while
keeping equality (12) true for i0 , which contradicts the minimality of τ ′ (using that τ ′ > θ). (It implies
that i0 is also payable with a smaller τ ′ , which contradicts the minimality of τ ′ .) To see this, define
             (i )

                       (i )                                                                                   (i )

     By the above calculations, we have

                                                           +
                                     j∈ A                                j∈ I

                               ≥ ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)] + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i0 )]+
                                                           +
                                     j∈ A                        j∈ I

                               > fˆ ,

which implies that τ ′ could have been further lowered while keeping (12) true for i0 , contradicting
the minimality of τ ′ . This concludes the proof that there is an openable facility, which proves the
lemma.

   In the next subsection we will merge two solutions into one with |Sreg | = k and Sfree = O(log n/ε2 ).
To our help, we will have two basic routines C OMPLETE S OLUTION and C OMPLETE S EQUENCE:
   • C OMPLETE S OLUTION f ,u (H) takes a partial solution H as input and returns a solution by run-
     ning the while-loop of stage 1 in L OG A DAPTIVE using the opening cost f and the distances u
     for free facilities already opened.

   • C OMPLETE S EQUENCE f ,u (H = ( H1 , . . . , H p−1 ), H p = ⟨h1 , . . . , hℓ ⟩) where H is partial solution
     and H p is an η-valid sequence except that it may not be maximal. C OMPLETE S EQUENCE then
     runs the while-loop of stage 1 in L OG A DAPTIVE starting with H p to return a maximal sequence
     whose prefix is H p (with respect to opening cost f and distances u).

We note that all facilities added by C OMPLETE S OLUTION and C OMPLETE S EQUENCE are 0-openable
and regular. Additionally, for intuition, observe that, with the above notation, the set of facilities
opened by C OMPLETE S OLUTION f ,u (∅) is equivalent to that opened by L OG A DAPTIVE. (There is no
free facility so u is meaningless here.) To simplify notation, we only use C OMPLETE S EQUENCE when
the partial solution H is clear from the context, so we simply write C OMPLETE S EQUENCE ( f ,u) (⟨h1 , . . . , hℓ ⟩).
Furthermore, we omit ( f , u) when the parameters are clear from the context.



                                                                   25
3.5     M ERGE S OLUTIONS
We now present our procedure M ERGE S OLUTIONS that walks between two solutions. We will main-
tain two solutions (H, f , u) and (H ′ , f ′ , u′ ) whose numbers of open regular facilities sandwich k. (For-
mally, let us say a and b sandwich k if a < k < b or b < k < a.) We will gradually make them closer
until one of them opens exactly k regular facilities. We start from the two initial solutions obtained
using the standard method. Note that the minimum nonzero distance is 1 and the maximum distance
is M = poly(n), which implies that opt ≤ nM.

Initialization. We consider executions of C OMPLETE S OLUTION f ,u (∅) with empty u. When f =
1/n2 , every client j ∈ D satisfies d( j, S) = 0 if d( j, F ) = 0 and d( j, S) ≤ d( j, F ) + 2/n2 otherwise, as
each client can open its closest facility by itself when α j = d( j, F ) + 2/n2 . If this solution opens at most
k centers, then it is already an (1 + 2/n)-approximation and we return it. On the other hand, when
f = 4nM, the algorithm will open exactly one facility, since the first facility is open when θ ≥ 2M,
so every client becomes immediately inactive when it is open. We then perform a binary search in
the interval [1/n2 , 4nM ] of values of f , keeping the invariant that for the current research interval
[ f , f ′ ] the corresponding two solutions (H, f , u) and (H′ , f ′ , u) are such that (H, f , u) opens less than
k facilities and (H ′ , f ′ , u) opens more than k facilities. We halt the process when f ≤ f ′ ≤ f + η.

General Step. M ERGE S OLUTIONS works on phase p = 1, 2, . . . iteratively, and ensures that the
two solutions are identical up to the p-th phase. Formally, M ERGE S OLUTIONS takes two solutions
(H, f , u) and (H′ , f ′ , u′ ) as well as the current phase p as input. The following promises on them will
be satisfied whenever we call M ERGE S OLUTIONS.
   • Both solutions consist of η-valid sequences.
   • The numbers of open regular facilities by the two solutions sandwich k.
   • Exactly one parameter, which we call the difference parameter, differs by at most η between the
     two solutions, i.e, if f ̸= f ′ then | f − f ′ | ≤ η and u(ĩ ) = u′ (ĩ ) for every ĩ ∈ Ffree ; otherwise if
     f = f ′ there is exactly one free facility ĩ ∈ Ffree so that u(ĩ ) ̸= u′ (ĩ ) and |u(ĩ ) − u′ (ĩ )| ≤ η.
   • H = ( H1 , H2 , . . . , HL ) and H′ = ( H1′ , H2′ , . . . , HL′ ′ ) have a common prefix of length p − 1,
     i.e., Hi = Hi′ for i = 1, 2, . . . , p − 1. Furthermore, the remaining sequences H p , . . . , HL and
     H p′ , . . . , HL′ ′ only contain regular facilities and are 0-valid with respect to the parameters f , u
     and f ′ , u′ , respectively. (This implies that H and H ′ open the same set of free facilities.)
      Given the promises, the goal of M ERGE S OLUTIONS is to
  (1) either find a solution of η-valid sequences that opens exactly k regular facilities and Oε (log n)
      free facilities, or
  (2) produce two new solutions that satisfy the above promises for phase p + 1.
Since the maximum distance between any two points is M ≤ poly(n) and f ≤ 4nM, before θ reaches
6M, at least one facility will open and every client will become inactive. Therefore, for some threshold
p∗ = O(log n/ε2 ), once H and H ′ agree on the first p∗ phases, they open exactly the same set of
facilities which violates the promise that the numbers of open regular facilities sandwich k. Therefore,
case (1) must happen for some p ≤ p∗ , which yields a solution of η-valid sequences that opens exactly
k regular facilities and O(log n) free facilities. Combined with Theorem 3.10, this proves Theorem 1.2.
Indeed, we then have a solution consisting of open centers S with |S| = k + O(log(n)/ε2 ) such that,
by Theorem 3.10,
                                      2
                     ∑ d( j, S) ≤ 1 − δ · (optLP ( f ) − ( f − ηn) · k) ≤ (2 + O(ε))opt ,
                     j∈ D

where, for the last inequality, we used that η = 2−n and opt ≥ 1 (since any distance is at least 1).
Theorem 1.2 then follows by scaling ε appropriately.
   In the remainder of this section, we present M ERGE S OLUTIONS for two solutions and a given
phase p. It consists of three subroutines, presented in Section 3.5.1 3.5.2, and 3.5.3 respectively. Each


                                                       26
subroutine achieves (1) or (2) above (which ends the whole procedure or lets us move on to the next
phase), or sets up the stage for the next subroutine. The final third subroutine always achieves (1) or
(2).

3.5.1   Making Parameters Identical
In this subsection, we try to make the parameters of the two solutions exactly identical. By renaming,
we assume that if f ̸= f ′ is the difference parameter then f ′ < f , and if the difference parameter is
u(ĩ ) ̸= u′ (ĩ ) then u(ĩ ) < u′ (ĩ ).
Claim 2. H1′ , H2′ , . . . , H p′ are η-valid sequences with respect to parameters f , u.

Proof. Suppose first that f ̸= f ′ is the difference parameter. As the first p − 1 sequences are identical,
we have that H1′ , . . . , H p′ −1 are η-valid with respect to parameters f , u. We further have that H p′ is η-
valid with respect to parameters f , u since it was 0-valid with respect to parameters f ′ , u′ and the only
difference is that f is slightly bigger than f ′ by at most η. For maximality, we have by Lemma 3.11
that there is no payable facilities after opening H p′ with respect to opening cost f ′ and increasing the
opening cost f cannot introduce any new payable facilities, so H p′ is also maximal with respect to f .
    Now assume that u(ĩ ) ̸= u′ (ĩ ) is the difference parameter. Then by the same arguments as above,
we have H1′ , . . . , H p′ −1 are η-valid with respect to f , u. Moreover, H p′ was 0-valid with respect to f ′ , u′
and we have that u′ (ĩ ) − η ≤ u(ĩ ) ≤ u′ (ĩ ). It remains to show that H p′ is η-valid with respect to f , u in
this case. The maximility of H p′ with respect to f , u is implied by Lemma 3.11 by the same arguments
as above when f ̸= f ′ : since lowering u(ĩ ) ≤ u′ (ĩ ) only decreases the payments toward opening
facilities and, hence, it does not introduce any new payable facilities. We proceed to show that the
facilities remain η-openable.
      Let H p′ = ⟨h1 , . . . , hℓ ⟩ and consider ht for some t ∈ [ℓ], which is 0-openable with respect to
f ′ , u′ , θ = (1 + ε2 ) p−1 and some superset S′ ⊇ (∪q∈[ p−1] Hq ) ∪ { h1 , . . . , ht−1 }. When we change from
f ′ , u′ to f , u, since the only difference is u(ĩ ) < u′ (ĩ ), the set of active clients A′ for f ′ , u′ is a superset
of the set of active clients A for f , u. Also, let d′ (·, ·) and d(·, ·) be the distances with respect to u′ and
u respectively. Based on the fact that ht was 0-openable with respect to the former setting with bids
(τj′ ) j∈ A′ , let (τj ) j∈ A be the new bids defined as τj = min(τj′ , (1 − δ)d( j, S)) for j ∈ A. Let us show that
ht was η-openable with respect to the latter setting with bids (τj ) j∈ A by checking the conditions in
Definition 3:

   • τj = α j for every j ∈ A − B(ht , εθ ): Since it was true for A′ ⊇ A, it remains true.

   • α j ≤ τj ≤ min{(1 − δ)d( j, S), (1 + ε2 )θ } for every j ∈ A ∩ B(ht , εθ ): Fix j ∈ A ∩ B(ht , εθ ). Since
     A ⊆ A′ , τj′ ≤ min{(1 − δ)d′ ( j, S), (1 + ε2 )θ }. Since τj is defined as min(τj′ , (1 − δ)d( j, S)), the
     condition is satisfied. (Note that j ∈ A implies α j ≤ min(τj′ , (1 − δ)d( j, S)) as well.)

   • ∑ j∈ A [τj − (1 − δ)d(ht , j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ j∈ I [d( j, S) − d(ht , j)]+ ≥ fˆ − nη: Let us argue that the
     contribution of each client j to the left-hand side changes by at most η between ( A′ , d′ , τ ′ ) and
     ( A, d, τ ). For j ∈ A, note that τj < τj′ implies τj = (1 − δ)d( j, ĩ ) ≥ (1 − δ)(d′ ( j, ĩ ) − η ) ≥ τj′ − η,
     so τj ≥ τj′ − η in any case. For j ∈ I ′ , note that d( j, S) and d′ ( j, S) only differ by at most η. Finally
     for j ∈ A′ ∩ I, (1 − δ)d( j, S) ≥ τj ≥ τj′ − η. Therefore, the fact that this condition was satisfied
     for ( A′ , d′ , τ ′ ) with fˆ on the right-hand side implies that ( A, d, τ ) satisfies this condition with
     fˆ − nη on the right-hand side.

   • For every regular facility i0 and ℓ ∈ A, ∑ j∈ A [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ j∈ I [τℓ − 2d( j, i0 ) − d(ℓ, i0 )]+ ≤ fˆ:
     Since ℓ ∈ A implies ℓ ∈ A′ , we have

                                ∑ ′ [τj′ − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑′ [τℓ′ − 2d( j, i0 ) − d(ℓ, i0 )]+ ≤ fˆ.
                               j∈ A                      j∈ I

        (Since i0 is a regular facility, d(i0 , j) for a client j does not change by changing u(ĩ ).) For j ∈
        A′ ∩ I, since j ∈ I and ℓ ∈ A, one has

                              τj′ ≥ α j ≥ d( j, S) ≥ d(ℓ, S) − d(ℓ, j) ≥ τℓ − d( j, i0 ) − d(ℓ, i0 ).

Outcome of this section of M ERGE S OLUTIONS.                              Let

                                   H′′ = C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( f ,u) ( H1′ , . . . , H p′ ) ,

which is a valid call by Claim 2. If H′′ opens k regular facilities, we return this solution. Otherwise, as
(H, f , u) and (H′ , f ′ , u′ ) sandwich k, we must have that either (H, f , u) and (H′′ , f , u), or (H′′ , f , u)
and (H′ , f ′ , u′ ) sandwich k. On the one hand, if (H′′ , f , u) and (H ′ , f ′ , u′ ) sandwich k, then we made
progress in that we now have two solutions with a common prefix of length p (instead of p − 1) and
only one parameter differs by at most η. In this case, we are done with phase p and can continue
M ERGE S OLUTIONS with these two solutions and phase p + 1.
   On the other hand, if (H, f , u) and (H ′′ , f , u) sandwich k, then these two solutions have the same
parameters and their first η-valid sequences that differ are H p and H p′ . In this case, we rename H ′′ to
H′ and proceed to the next step.

3.5.2    Maximize Common Prefix of H p and H p′

We are given two solutions (H, f , u) and (H′ , f , u) on the same parameters f , u that sandwich k.
Moreover, if we let H = ( H1 , . . . , HL ) and H′ = ( H1′ , . . . , HL′ ′ ), then Hi = Hi′ for i = 1, . . . , p − 1 and
H p ̸= H p′ . In addition H p and H p′ only contain regular facilities.
    In this step of M ERGE S OLUTIONS, we increase the common prefix of H p and H p′ by “slowly”
modifying H p′ . Recall that H p only contains regular facilities: let H p = ⟨i1 , i2 , . . . , iℓ ⟩. Moreover, as
the parameters ( f , u) are clear from the context, we omit them in the following when, e.g., calling the
procedures C OMPLETE S OLUTION and C OMPLETE S EQUENCE.

  Transforming H p′ step-by-step so that H p becomes a prefix of H p′ .

  Repeat the following until all of H p becomes a prefix of H p′ .

        • Let q be the largest index so that H p′ = ⟨i1 , i2 , . . . , iq , h′q+1 , . . . , h′ℓ ⟩, i.e., q equals the length
                                                                                               1
          of the common prefix i1 , i2 , . . . , iq of H p and H p′ .

        • Update H p′ by inserting a free copy of iq+1 , ĩq+1 , at the end of H p′ with u(ĩq+1 ) = 0. That is,
          we get the sequence

                                              H p′ = ⟨i1 , i2 , . . . , iq , h′q+1 , . . . , h′ℓ1 , ĩq+1 ⟩, .

        • For t = q + 1, . . . , ℓ1 ,
              – Update H p′ by first removing h′t and then completing the sequence by a call to C OM -
                PLETE S EQUENCE. More explicitly, if

                                    H p′ =⟨i1 , . . . , iq , h′t , h′t+1 . . . , h′ℓ1 , ĩq+1 , h′ℓ1 +1 , . . . , h′ℓ2 ⟩




                                                                        28
              then we delete h′t to obtain

                               H p′′ =⟨i1 , . . . , iq , h′t+1 . . . , h′ℓ1 , ĩq+1 , h′ℓ1 +1 , . . . , h′ℓ2 ⟩

              and finally update H p′ = C OMPLETE S EQUENCE ( H p′′ ) which will be of the form

                                        ⟨i1 , . . . , iq , h′t+1 , . . . , h′ℓ1 , ĩq+1 , h′ℓ1 +1 , . . . , h′ℓ2 , . . . , h′ℓ3 ⟩ ,

              where h′ℓ2 +1 , . . . , h′ℓ3 are the facilities added by C OMPLETE S EQUENCE (which could be
              empty).
      • After removing h′q+1 , . . . , h′ℓ , we have
                                            1


                                            H p′ = ⟨i1 , i2 , . . . , iq , ĩq+1 , h′x , . . . , h′y ⟩ .

         We update H p′ by replacing ĩq+1 with its regular copy iq+1 , i.e., we obtain

                                            H p′ = ⟨i1 , i2 , . . . , iq , iq+1 , h′x , . . . , h′y ⟩ .

We have the following claims.
Claim 3. Throughout the updates to H p′ , H1′ , . . . , H p′ remains an η-valid sequence, and H p′ contains at most
one free facility.

Proof. As we never update f or the value u(·) of a free facility in H1′ , . . . , H p′ −1 , these sequences remain
η-valid.
   We continue to argue that H p′ remains η-valid throughout its updates. By Claim 2, this is true
before any updates to H p′ . We now analyze the three types of updates to H p′ .

   • Updating H p′ by inserting a free copy ĩq+1 of iq+1 maintains that it is η-valid as every reg-
     ular facility stays η-openable in the sequence (they have the same prefix as before) and the
     sequence remains maximal (by Lemma 3.11 as adding a free facility does not make any new
     facility payable).
   • Updating H p′ by removing h′t and calling C OMPLETE S EQUENCE. Removing a h′t can only de-
     crease the prefix of regular facilities in the sequence, and so each regular facility still has a
     (superset) of facilities for which it is η-openable. Finally, the call C OMPLETE S EQUENCE ensures
     that the sequence is maximal.

   • Updating H p′ by replacing ĩq+1 with its regular copy iq+1 . First note that, since u(ĩq+1 ) = 0, this
     does not impact on the following regular facilities being η-openable (with respect to a superset).
     By the same argument, the sequence stays maximal (as ĩq+1 with u(ĩq+1 ) = 0 and iq+1 has the
     same impact on following facilities). Finally, to see that iq+1 is η-openable (with respect to a
     potential superset) notice that the prefix i1 , . . . , iq is the same as that in H p which is an η-valid
     sequence.

   We complete the proof of the claim by arguing that H p′ contains at most one free facility at any
point of time. This follows from the facts that (1) H p′ initially contains no free facility, (2) in one
repetition, the free facility ĩq+1 is inserted into H p′ , and (3) ĩq+1 is replaced by its regular copy iq+1
before continuing to the next repetition.

Outcome of this section of M ERGE S OLUTIONS.                          We distinguish three cases.

Case 1. If at some point H p′ is such that C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1′ , H2′ , . . . , H p′ ) opens exactly k reg-
ular facilities, then we return that solution.



                                                                    29
Case 2. Otherwise, suppose there is an update to H p′ so that if we let H pbefore and H pafter denote H p′
before and after this update, respectively, then C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pbefore ) and C OM -
PLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pafter ) sandwich k. There are three kinds of updates to H p′ . However,
note that the update where ĩq+1 is replaced by its regular copy iq+1 cannot satisfy the conditions of
this case. Indeed, as u(ĩq+1 ) = 0, we have

             C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pbefore ) = C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pafter )

in this case, which contradicts that they sandwich k.
     We proceed to analyze the two other kind of updates. If the update to H p′ is to insert a free facility
ĩq+1 with u(ĩq+1 ) = 0. Then

                                         H pbefore = ⟨i1 , i2 , . . . , iq , h′q+1 , . . . , h′ℓ1 ⟩

Note that the solution C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pbefore ) is equivalent to C OMPLETE S OLU -

M ERGE S OLUTIONS with the two solutions and phase p + 1, since they have the first p, instead of p − 1,
η-valid sequences in common and exactly one difference parameter. Their maximality follows from
that of H pbefore .
   Finally, consider when the update to H p′ is of the second type, i.e., u(ĩq+1 ) = 0 and

                          H pbefore =⟨i1 , . . . , iq , h′t , h′t+1 . . . , h′ℓ1 , ĩq+1 , h′ℓ1 +1 , . . . , h′ℓ2 ⟩

In this case, C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pbefore ) and C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pafter ) are two
solutions with the same parameters f , u, that sandwich k and we proceed to the next step with the
following properties of H pbefore and H pafter :

   • Both sequences contain one free facility, ĩq+1 .

   • H pbefore and H pafter are identical except that (1) H pbefore contains a regular facility h′t not present
     in H pafter , and (2) H pafter contains a (potentially empty) suffix of regular facilities not present in
     H pbefore .

Case 3. None of the previous cases apply. Then, completing the solution with the initial H p′ and
the final H p′ cannot sandwich k. Since then (assuming the first case does not apply) there must be
an update to H p′ that results in two solutions that sandwich k: this happens because the completion
of H p and the completion of the initial H p′ sandwich k. The above implies that if we let H pbefore =
H p and H pafter equal the final H p′ then C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pbefore ) and C OMPLETE S OLU -
TION ( H1 , . . . , H pafter ) sandwich k. Further note that, since H pafter equals H p′ after the final updates,
H pbefore appears as a prefix of H pafter and both sequences only contain regular facilities. To summarize,
in this case, C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pbefore ) and C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pafter ) are two
solutions with the same parameters f , u, that sandwich k and we proceed to the next step with the
following properties of H pbefore and H pafter :
   • Both sequences contain no free facilities.
   • H pbefore and H pafter are identical except that H pafter contains a suffix of regular facilities not present
     in H pbefore .


                                                                          30
3.5.3    Removing Extra Facilities
The input to this step consists of two solutions C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pbefore ) and C OM -
PLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pafter ) that have the same parameters f , u and sandwich k. Moreover, H pbefore
and H pafter both contain at most one free facility and they are identical except for potentially two dif-
ferences:
   • H pbefore may contain a facility not present in H pafter ;

   • H pafter contains a suffix of regular facilities (which may be empty) that is not present in H pbefore .

   To simplify the notation in this section, we let i∗ denote the regular facility in H pbefore that is not
present in H pafter if it exists (so i∗ corresponds to h′t in the previous section). We further let h1 , . . . , hℓ
denote the suffix of H pafter not present in H pbefore .
   We now transform H pafter into H pbefore one change at a time:

  Generation of η-valid sequences from H pafter to H pbefore .

        • If H pbefore contains the regular facility i∗ not present in H pafter , then let ĩ∗ be a free copy of
          i∗ with u(ĩ∗ ) = 0 and obtain H p0 from H pafter by adding ĩ∗ at the end. Otherwise, we let H p0
          equal H pafter .

        • For r = 1, . . . , ℓ, obtain H pr from H pr−1 by removing hr .

We remark that H pℓ is identical to H pbefore since we removed the whole suffix h1 , . . . hℓ present in H pafter ,
except for potentially the facility i∗ . That is, the only difference in H pbefore and H pℓ may be the place-
ment of ĩ∗ in the sequence and the fact that ĩ∗ is a free copy of the regular facility i∗ present in H pbefore .
However, since u(ĩ∗ ) = 0, we have that the two sequences H pbefore and H pℓ have an identical impact
on C OMPLETE S OLUTION, which is a fact we will use in the proof of Claim 5.
Claim 4. For r = 0, 1 . . . ℓ, H pr is an η-valid sequence with at most two free facilities.

Proof. The sequence H pr has at most two free facilities because H pafter has at most one free facility, and
we potentially add one new free facility ĩ∗ .
     We now verify that H pr is η-valid. For any regular facility h′ in the sequence, the prefix of H pr before
h′ is a subset of the prefix before h′ in H pafter . Thus, as h′ was openable with respect to a superset S(h′ )
in H pafter , h′ must be η-openable with respect to the same superset S(h′ ) in H pr . This ensures that the
first property of Definition 4 is satisfied. To verify maximality, notice that since we added ĩt with
u(ĩt ) = 0 in the end, we have that the set of active clients after opening the facilities in H pr is a subset
of the active clients after opening the facilities in H pbefore . Hence, the maximality of H pbefore implies the
maximality of H pr (by Lemma 3.11 as any payable facility in H pr is also payable in H pbefore ).
Claim 5. One of the following is true.
   • For one of the sequences H pr , C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pr ) opens k regular facilities.
                                                 after
   • C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H p         ) and C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H p0 ) sandwich k.

   • C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pr ) and C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pr+1 ) sandwich k
     for some r ∈ {0, 1, . . . , ℓ − 1}.

Proof. Assume the first bullet point does not hold, i.e., the completion of no H pr leads to the opening
of k regular facilities. Now notice that the second and third bullet points ask whether consecutive
solutions sandwich k, starting with H pafter and ending with H pℓ . By definition, H pℓ equals H pbefore except


                                                             31
potentially for the placement of ĩ∗ and that ĩ∗ is the free copy of i∗ present in H pbefore . However, as
u(ĩt ) = 0, the two sequences have an identical impact when we complete the solution, which implies

              C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pℓ ) = C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pbefore ).

Thus, as C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pafter ) and C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , . . . , H pbefore ) sandwich
k, either the second or third bullet point of the statement must be valid (since no solution opened
exactly k regular facilities).

Outcome of this section of M ERGE S OLUTIONS.                We distinguish three cases based on the first bullet
point that is true in Claim 5.

Case 1. If one of the sequences H pr is such that C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pr ) opens k regu-
lar facilities, then we return that solution.

Case 2. Otherwise, if the solutions C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pafter ) and C OMPLETE S OLU -
TION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H p0 ) sandwich k, then H p0 is equal to H pafter except that ĩ p was added in the end of
H pafter with u(ĩ p ) = 0. Note that the solution C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pafter ) is equivalent
to C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H p0 ) if we set u(ĩq+1 ) = 10M (except the presence of ĩ∗ ). We can
thus do binary search on u(ĩ∗ ) to find two values u′ (ĩ∗ ) and u(ĩ∗ ) with |u′ (ĩ∗ ) − u(ĩ∗ )| ≤ η so that
the two solutions C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( f ,u′ ) ( H1 , . . . , H p0 ) and C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( f ,u) ( H1 , . . . , H p0 )
sandwich k (where u and u′ are identical except for ĩq+1 ). We have thus finished phase p, and we can
restart M ERGE S OLUTIONS with the two solutions and phase p + 1, since they have the first p, instead
of p − 1, η-valid sequences in common and exactly one difference parameter.

Case 3. Finally, consider the case when none of the above cases apply and thus (by Claim 5) there is
an r ∈ {0, 1, . . . , ℓ − 1} so that C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pr ) and
C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pr+1 ) sandwich k. The only difference between H pr and H pr+1 is
that H pr contains hr+1 which is not present in H r+1 . Let H p be the sequence obtained by adding a free
copy ĩr+1 of hr+1 at the end of H r+1 (which clearly maintains that is η-valid), then

C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H p ) = C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pr ) if u(ĩr+1 ) = 0

and

C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H p ) = C OMPLETE S OLUTION ( H1 , H2 , . . . , H pr+1 ) if u(ĩr+1 ) = 10M.

Hence, doing a binary search on u(ĩr+1 ) as in the previous case finishes phase p. We can restart
M ERGE S OLUTIONS with the two solutions and phase p + 1, since they have the first p, instead of
p − 1, η-valid sequences in common and exactly one difference parameter.


4     (2 + ε)-Approximation for ( logζ n )-Stable Instances
In this section, we give a (2 + ε)-approximation algorithm for ( logζ n )-stable k-median instances. The
proof of the theorem is presented in Section 4.7. We restate the theorem for the reader’s convenience.


Theorem 1.3. For any two constants ε, ζ > 0, there exists a randomized polynomial-time algorithm that, given
a (ζ/ log n)-stable k-median instance, returns a solution of cost at most (2 + ε)opt with high probability.
    To simplify notation, we let ε be the minimum of ε and ζ in the above theorem and further assume
that ε < 1/12 is sufficiently small. With this notation, we present a (2 + O(ε))-approximation algo-
rithm for ε/ log(n)-stable instances. This implies the above theorem as any ζ/ log(n)-stable instance
is also ζ ′ / log(n)-stable with ζ ′ ≤ ζ.


                                                            32
    The algorithm and the proof of the above theorem follow several steps where we iteratively sim-
plify the instance by adding more structure. Finally, we obtain enough structure to solve the problem
by maximizing a submodular function subject to a matroid partition constraint. We have not opti-
mized constants in favor of simplifying the description, and throughout this section we let Oε (log n)
denote O(log(n)/εO(1) ). An overview of this section and the algorithm is as follows:

  Overview of 2-Approximation for k-median on (ε/ log n)- Stable Instance k, D, F, d.

     1. We start, in Section 4.1, by running a standard Local Search on (k, D, F, d) to obtain a set of
        centers S. It is well-known that S is a constant-factor approximation. Moreover, for a stable
        instance, we show that S “correctly” implicitly identifies all but Oε (log n) centers of OPT.
        Specifically, this ensures that all but Oε (log n) clusters have the same cost as in OPT up to
        a (1 + O(ε)) factor.
     2. In Section 4.2, we use a technique inspired from the classic k-means++ algorithm and its
        so called D2 -sampling procedure to sample clients proportional to their cost in solution S,
        and we show that if we take Oε (log n) samples W then with good probability we “hit” all
        but an insignificant fraction of clusters of high cost in S.

     3. In Section 4.3, we then, guess a subset of the sampled clients W so that each hit cluster
        has exactly one client, which we refer to as the leader of that cluster. In addition, for each
        client ℓ that is a leader, we guess the (approximate) distance from ℓ to the optimal center in
        the optimal cluster it hits. This defines a set B of balls with the guarantee that the optimal
        solution opens one center in each of these balls. Moreover, if we let OPT   ˆ be the optimal
        centers corresponding to these balls, we prove in Section 4.3.1 that there is a swap of centers
        MO = S − S0 ∪ OPT    ˆ that removes S0 and adds the optimal centers OPT  ˆ so that the cost of
        the solution MO is good and in particular a 2 + O(ε)-approximation.
     4. The remaining steps are then focused on approximating this swap, i.e., intuitively to that
                           ˆ
        of finding S0 and OPT:

         (a) We first find the set Q of expensive centers of S0 so that the centers S0 − Q has to-
             tal cost O(εopt) in Section 4.4. This allows us to think of the centers in S0 − Q as
             contracted, i.e., as single weighted points, and in particular, that all clients of these
             clusters are assigned to the same center when their center in S0 is removed. We then,
             in Section 4.4.1, use this structure to partition S0 − Q into two sets U and R where U
             are those centers that are reassigned to other centers in S − S0 and R are those centers
                                     ˆ
             that are assigned to OPT.
         (b) The set U is then approximated with a set Ue (with close to identical properties) in
             Section 4.5.
         (c) At this stage, we have thus “guessed” all centers Q ∪ Ue to remove from S except for
             those in R. This is achieved in Section 4.6 where we reduce the problem of both find-
             ing R and an approximate version of OPT ˆ to that of submodular function optimization
             subject to a partition matroid (since we should open one center per ball in B ).

    We remark that in each of Steps 3, 4a, 4b, and 4c, we will make guesses by enumerating polyno-
          −O(1)
mially nε       many potential choices. In the following, we describe the algorithm and its analysis
by presenting each of these steps. When presenting the next step, we analyze the branch of the al-
gorithm that took the successful guesses up to that point. The algorithm outputs the solution of the
                             −O(1)
smallest cost among the nε          constructed solutions obtained by enumerating all possible guesses
(see Section 4.7 for a more formal treatment where we put everything together). In particular, the
final solution will have cost at most the solution that is output in the analyzed branch of “correct”
guesses.
    We end this section by introducing basic definitions and concepts, followed by the definition of
matroids and submodular functions. We also state the known result, an (1 − 1/e)-approximation
algorithm for maximizing monotone submodular functions subject to a matroid constraint, that we
use in the last step (Section 4.6).


                                                   33
Definitions of leaders. We use the following definitions throughout this section. Given a set of
centers A ⊆ F, we let cost( A) := ∑ p∈ D d( p, A) denote the cost of the corresponding clustering (where
each client p is assigned to the closest center in A and pays the corresponding distance). Sometimes
we will consider a (possibly suboptimal) assignment µ : D → A of clients to centers in A, and let
cost( A, µ) := ∑ p∈ D d( p, µ( p)) be the corresponding cost. Let OPT be a fixed optimum solution for
the k-median instance, and let opt = cost(OPT) be its cost. For a client p ∈ D, let OPT p := d( p, OPT)
be the cost paid by p in OPT.
    Let C ∗ be an OPT cluster with center c∗ ∈ OPT. We let avgC∗ ,OPT be the distance from c∗ to the
ε|C |th closest client of C ∗ to c∗ . We further let Cavg
   ∗                                                  ∗ be the set of clients of C ∗ at a distance at most
avgC∗ ,OPT from c , we refer to these clients as leaders for C ∗ . So avgC∗ ,OPT is the maximum distance
                   ∗

from a leader to its center c∗ . Also note that we have, |Cavg
                                                            ∗ | ≥ ε |C ∗ |. We thus have that avg
                                                                                                  C ∗ ,OPT is
                      1                             1              ∗                           ∗
upper bounded by 1−ε times the average cost |C∗ | ∑ p∈C∗ d( p, c ), and at the same time Cavg contains
a constant fraction of the clients.

Partition matroids. In Section 4.6, we formulate a submodular function that we then maximize over
a partition matroid using known results. We define those concepts and also state the result that we
will use. First, recall the definition of a matroid and partition matroid. A matroid is a tuple ( E, I)
defined on a ground set E with a family of independent sets I that satisfy: (1) if A ∈ I and A′ ⊆ A,
then A′ ∈ I ; and (2) if A, B ∈ I and | A| < | B|, then there exists an element e ∈ B − A such that
A ∪ {e} ∈ I . In the special case of a partition matroid, the ground set E is partitioned into disjoint
subsets E1 , E2 , . . . , Ek , and there are non-negative integers r1 , r2 , . . . , rk such that:

   • A subset I ⊆ E is independent if and only if | I ∩ Ei | ≤ ri for each i = 1, 2, . . . , k.
In other words, each subset Ei has a capacity limit ri , and an independent set I cannot contain more
than ri elements from Ei . To work with matroids (to get a running time that is polynomial in the
size of the ground set E), we often use independence queries, which allow us to determine if a given
subset A ⊆ E is independent (i.e., whether A ∈ I ). Specifically, an independence query on a subset
A returns true if A is independent and false otherwise.

Submodular functions. Having defined matroids, we proceed to define non-negative monotone
submodular functions. For a finite ground set E, a set function f : 2E → R is submodular if it satisfies
the diminishing returns property: for every pair of sets A ⊆ B ⊆ E and any element e ∈ E − B, it
holds that
                             f ( A ∪ {e}) − f ( A) ≥ f ( B ∪ {e}) − f ( B).
Intuitively, this means that adding an element e to a smaller set A provides at least as much additional
value as adding e to a larger set B. In addition, f is called monotone if for any pair of sets A ⊆ B ⊆ E,
we have f ( A) ≤ f ( B). In other words, adding elements to a set does not decrease the function value.
Finally, f is non-negative if f ( A) ≥ 0 for all A ⊆ E.
   A celebrated result [CCPV11] gives a polynomial-time (1 − 1/e)-approximation algorithm for the
problem of maximizing a non-negative monotone submodular function over a matroid constraint.
While the original result was a randomized algorithm, a recent striking result [BF24], building upon
the work of [FW14], gives a deterministic algorithm with the same guarantee. We remark that poly-
nomial time here refers to a running time that is polynomial in the size of the ground set E. To
summarize, they show the following theorem.
Theorem 4.1. Let f : 2E → R be a non-negative monotone submodular function that we can evaluate in
polynomial time, and let ( E, I) be a matroid on the same groundset, for which we can answer independence
queries in polynomial time. Then, for every ζ > 0, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that outputs a set
X ⊆ E with X ∈ I so that
                                    f ( X ) ≥ (1 − 1/e − ζ ) · max f ( X ∗ ) .
                                                              X ∗ ∈I

4.1     Properties of a Locally Optimal Solution
Let S be a locally optimal solution output by the following standard local search algorithm4 . We
remark that its running time is polynomial since by Lemma 1.4 the cost of a solution is a polynomially
bounded integer and, at each improving step, the cost decreases by at least one.

  localSearch(·)

      1: S ← arbitrary solution for k-Median
      2: while there exists a solution S′ such that |S∆S′ | ≤ 2 and cost(S′ ) < cost(S ) do
      3:    S ← S′
      4: return S

Let S be the solution returned by localSearch(). Local search achieves a 5 approximation for k-median,
see e.g. Chapter 9.2 in [WS11].

Lemma 4.2. cost(S) ≤ 5 · opt.
   In the remaining part of the algorithm and its analysis we fix S, and recall that we fixed the ref-

properties in the next two sections. We let S p be the cost paid by p in S and recall that OPT p denotes
the cost p ∈ D pays in OPT.

4.2        Cheap Clusters and D-Sample Process to Hit Expensive Clusters
In the previous section, we showed that the cost of pure clusters in OPT is close to to their optimal
cost in our local search solution S. Our goal in this section is to “hit” those non-pure clusters of OPT
that have a high cost in S. We say that a non-pure cluster C ∗ of OPT with center c∗ is basic-cheap if the
                                   ∗ is less than ε5 opt/ log n or if there exists a center of S at distance at
total cost in S of the clients in Cavg

say that C ∗ is covered by S.
    The following procedure, that aims to “hit” all clusters of OPT that are non-pure and non-basic-
cheap, is inspired by the classic k-means++ algorithm and its so-called D2 -sampling procedure to
sample clients proportional to their cost in solution S. For k-median this becomes D-sampling (instead
of D2 -sampling).

4.3        Identifying Balls of Expensive Clusters
By the definition of expensive clusters, each expensive cluster C ∗ of OPT satisfies C ∗ ∩ W ̸= ∅ where
W is the sample obtained by running the sampling procedure of the last subsection. Here, our goal
is to guess a subset B of balls so that for each expensive cluster C ∗ there is a ball B(ℓ, ρ) ∈ B so that

approximate the centers in OPT  ˆ via submodular function maximization subject to a partition matroid
constraint (the balls will correspond to the partitions of the matroid). As the algorithm does not know
the set of expensive clusters, it enumerates all subsets of W, and for each guessed point, it enumerates
a constant number of radii.

   ˆ S0 ) would provide. Of course, it will not necessarily be optimum, but we can show it is enough

use of the following procedure that takes as input the local search solution S and the set Λ of dummy

  expRem(S, Λ)

      1: Lexp ← ∅
      2: for ( 8ε )|Λ|+1 ln n many iterations do
      3:     Q←∅
      4:     for |Λ| + 1 many iterations do
      5:           With probability 1/2 do the following. Consider the clustering induced by Λ ∪ S − Q
         and, for each c ∈ S − Q, let cost(c) be the cost of the cluster associated with c in the considered
         solution

                                                                     c ∈S−Q
      7:        Set Q ← Q ∪ {c}
      8:     Lexp ← Lexp ∪ {Q}

The goal of the above procedure is to guess a subset Q of all “costly” centers in S0 (the set Lexp
contains all guesses). Specifically, if we consider the solution (S − Q) ∪ Λ, then we wish that the
cost of the clusters corresponding to the remaining centers in S0 − Q is at most ε · opt. Formally,


                                                             42
the clusters corresponding to S0 − Q in S − Q ∪ Λ consists of all the clients whose closest center in
S − Q ∪ Λ is from S0 − Q. If we let D ′ ⊆ D be those clients then the total cost in solution S − Q ∪ Λ
of the clusters with centers in S0 − Q is defined as

                                  ∑ d( p, S − Q ∪ Λ) = ∑ ′ d( p, S0 − Q) ,
                                 p∈ D ′                     p∈ D

where the equality holds because of the definition of D ′ .
                                          O (1)                                                     O (1)
Lemma 4.11. expRem() runs in n1/ε           time and produces a collection Lexp of at most n1/ε             subsets of S
such that with probability at least 1 − 1/n there exists Q ∈ Lexp satisfying
   1. Q ⊆ S0 , and
   2. The total cost in the solution S − Q ∪ Λ of the clusters with centers in S0 − Q is at most ε · opt.
Proof. The claim on the running time and on the size of Lexp is trivially satisfied since |Λ| = |S0 | ≤
log n
  ε3
      .
   It remains to show that with probability at least 1 − n1 at least one set Q ∈ Lexp satisfies the desired
properties. Let us consider some iteration of the external for loop, and let Q be the corresponding set.
We say that the j-th iteration of the corresponding inner for loop is successful if the following happens:
   1. If condition (2) of the lemma is satisfied considering the current value of Q, then the event from
      line 5 does not happen (hence in particular Q is not updated in this j-th iteration).
   2. Otherwise, the event from line 5 happens and furthermore the sampled c belongs to S0 .
Let A j denote the event that the considered j-th iteration is successful. Let us condition on the event
A< j that the previous iterations A1 , . . . , A j−1 are successful. In particular, one has Q ⊆ S0 at the
beginning of the j-th iteration. Let Bj denote the event that condition (2) is satisfied at the beginning
of the iteration. Then trivially Pr [ A j | Bj , A< j ] ≥ 1/2. Suppose next that Bj does not hold. In that case,
with probability 1/2, the procedure samples a center c. Since by assumption ∑c∈S0 −Q cost(c) > εopt,
the probability that the procedure samples some c ∈ S0 − Q is at least
                            εopt                 εopt              εopt         ε
                                         ≥                 ≥                   ≥ ,
                      ∑c′ ∈S−Q cost(c′ )   cost(Λ ∪ S − Q)   cost(Λ ∪ S − S0 )  4

where in the last inequality we used the fact that cost(Λ ∪ S − S0 ) ≤ 4 · opt by Lemma 4.9.
    Altogether Pr [ A j | B j , A< j ] ≥ ε/8. Thus Pr [ A j | A< j ] ≥ ε/8. Chaining the obtained inequalities we
obtain that all the events A1 , . . . , A|S0 |+1 are simultaneously true with probability at least (ε/8)|S0 |+1 .
Notice that when the latter event happens, the corresponding Q satisfies all the properties. Indeed,
Q ⊆ S0 and furthermore the event Bj must happen for some j ≤ |S0 | + 1 (hence for the next iterations
if any) since otherwise Q would contain more than |S0 | elements.
                                                                                              |S |+1 ln n
    Thus, the probability that the overall procedure fails is at most (1 − ( 8ε )|S0 |+1 )(8/ε) 0             ≤ n1 .

4.4.1     Consistently Assigning Clients in Mixed Solutions and the sets U and R
While our algorithm tries all possible Q ∈ Lexp , we focus on the execution path of a set Q of centers
satisfying the properties of Lemma 4.11, i.e.,
   1. Q ⊆ S0 , and
   2. the cost in the solution S − Q ∪ Λ of the clusters with centers in S0 − Q is at most ε · opt.
We define the solution SQ := S − Q ∪ Λ and for a center c ∈ SQ we let SQ (c) be the subset of clients
closest to c (i.e., assigned to c) in the solution SQ . The second property above allows us to consider
each cluster SQ (c), c ∈ S0 − Q, as contracted by paying a small extra cost of ε · opt. In particular,
this allows us to give a “consistent” assignment µO of clients in the mixed solutions. Here, consistent
means that all clients in SQ (c), for c ∈ S0 − Q, are assigned to the same center in the mixed solutions
where all of S0 is removed.


                                                       43
Definition of µO and µ D . We modify how the clients are assigned in the solution MO to obtain
the assignment µO . For every c ∈ S0 − Q, all the clients in SQ (c) are reassigned to the same center
of MO as follows. Let c1 be the center of OPT   ˆ that is the closest to c and let c2 be the center of
MO − OPT ˆ = S − S0 that is the closest to c. The clients of SQ (c) are all assigned to either c1 or c2 :

   • If d(c, c1 ) ≤ d(c, c2 )/2, assign SQ (c) to c1 ; SQ (c) is called a type-1 cluster.
   • Otherwise (d(c, c1 ) > d(c, c2 )/2), assign SQ (c) to c2 ; SQ (c) is called a type-2 cluster.
Let T1 be the set of clients in a type-1 cluster and T2 the set of clients in a type-2 cluster. The remaining
             ∗ − ( T ∪ T ) are assigned to their closest centers in OPT,
clients in Dexp                                                             ˆ and the remaining clients in
                       1    2
           ∗
( D − Dexp ) − ( T1 ∪ T2 ) are assigned to their closest centers in S − S0 . Notice that, even neglecting the
extra cost due to the consistent assignment, the above assignment of clients in T2 is suboptimal when
d(c, c2 ) > d(c, c1 ) > d(c, c2 )/2. The motivation for this reassignment is technical. More specifically,
it will be used in Lemma 4.12 to have a better upper bound on the cost of clients in C ∗ ∩ T1 for a
non-expensive cluster C ∗ (case b2). This leads to a larger upper bound on the cost of clients in C ∗ ∩ T2
for an expensive cluster C ∗ (case a3), which is however tolerable.
    We further obtain an assignment µ D of clients in MD by modifying µO as follows: each client p
with µO ( p) ∈ OPT ˆ is assigned to the associated dummy center by µ D . So for each client p we have
µ D ( p) = µO ( p) unless µO ( p) ∈ OPTˆ in which case µ D ( p) equals the dummy center associated with
µO ( p ) .

Sets R and U . By the definition of type-1 and type-2 cluster, we can split the set of centers of S0 − Q
into two groups R and U . Let R be the set of centers of S0 − Q whose set of clients is completely
assigned to a center of OPT ˆ in the assignment µO , and let U = S0 − Q − R be the remaining ones that
are assigned to centers in S − S0 .
    We end this section by analyzing the costs of the assignments µO and µ D . Recall that we use
the notation cost( MO , µO ) to denote the cost of MO equipped with assignment µO and, similarly
cost( MD , µ D ) for the cost of MD equipped with assignment µ D . We say that the selection of (W, B , Q)
is successful, if the sample W selected in 4.2 is successful, the set of balls B from Section 4.3 is valid,
and Q selected in this section satisfies the properties of Lemma 4.11.
Lemma 4.12. If the selection of (W, B , Q) is successful,
                             cost( MO , µO ) + cost( MD , µ D ) ≤ 4 · opt + O(ε · opt) .

      center to p in S − S0 . Then

            d(c, c1 ) ≤ d(c, c2 )/2 ≤ d(c, c3 )/2 ≤ (d(c, p) + d( p, c3 ))/2 = (d( p, SQ ) + d( p, c3 ))/2.

     S − S0 , and so µO ( p) = µ D ( p). Now by Lemma 4.10 and the triangle inequality,

      d( p, µO ( p)) ≤ d( p, c) + d(c, c2 ) ≤ 2d( p, c) + d( p, S − S0 ) ≤ 2d( p, c) + r ( p) = 2d( p, SQ ) + r ( p).

      So we get the bound

                         d( p, µO ( p)) + d( p, µ D ( p)) = 2d( p, µO ( p)) ≤ 4d( p, SQ ) + 2r ( p) .

4.5   Removal of Cheap Centers in S0
At this stage we have defined a clustering SQ = S − Q ∪ Λ, where Λ is the set of dummy centers.
Recall that the mixed solution MO equals S − S0 ∪ OPT.ˆ So SQ − Λ represents progress compared to
S in that we have already removed a subset Q of S0 . Furthermore, with respect to the assignment of
clients µO , we have that the remaining centers S0 − Q, which we want to remove, are divided into two
sets R and U . The clusters with centers in R are completely assigned (by µO ) to centers in OPT,  ˆ and
every cluster with center in U is completely assigned to a center in S − S0 . The task of this section is to
make further progress by guessing the centers in U and their reassignment to centers in S − S0 . This


                                                          46
turns out to be a difficult task, and we will instead “approximately” guess a set Ue and reassignment
e of the clients of those clusters. Specifically, we will output a solution SQ∪Ue = SQ − Ue (obtained by
µ
removing Ue from SQ ) and an assignment µ     e of clients to centers in SQ − Ue. This is done through the
following lemma. Recall that for c ∈ SQ , SQ (c) is the set of clients assigned to c in SQ , i.e., closest to c
among all centers in SQ .
Lemma 4.13. Given S and Q as described above, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that produces a collection

             e( p) = c′ for every p ∈ SQ (c).

Proof of Lemma 4.13. Consider the execution of the above procedure for the correct guess of the values
|R|, |X | and |U |. We remark that, when line 7 is executed, the set S − Q − R′ − X ′ − N − Ue is
not empty (so that we can choose an appropriate c). Indeed, at each recursive call we add at most
two elements to R′ ∪ X ′ ∪ N ∪ Ue and the value of |U ′ | + |R′ | + |X ′ | + |Ue | grows by at least 1. The
latter value cannot grow more than 2|U | + |R| + |X | ≤ 2ℓ times since at that point we would have
necessarily |Ue | = |U |, which makes the condition of line 5 true. Since the condition of line 1 cannot
hold if line 7 is executed at least once, we have that |S − Q| > 4ℓ. Thus S − Q contains sufficiently
many elements to remove up to 2 elements for up to 2ℓ times.
    Concerning the running time of the procedure, each recursive step involves a branching on at
most 4 subproblems, and in each one of them the value of |U ′ | + |R′ | + |X ′ | + |Ue | grows by at least
1. Thus, by the same argument as before, the depth of the recursion is at most 2ℓ. So the number of
                                      O (1)
recursive calls is at most 42ℓ = n1/ε , implying a polynomial running time.
    It remains to show that at least one set Ue ∈ Lcheap (with the associated µ̃) at the end of the proce-
dure satisfies the claim. If the condition of line 1 holds, it must happen that in one of the executions
of line 3 one has Ue = U and R  e = R. In that case Ue and the corresponding µ    e trivially satisfy the claim.
In particular we remark that for each p ∈ SQ (c) with c ∈ U = U , µ
                                                              e        e( p) = next(c) = µO ( p).
