                                                          51
4.6   Finding S∗ via Submodular Optimization
In this section, we give a polynomial-time algorithm for finding the solution S∗ by reducing the
problem to maximizing a submodular function subject to a partition matroid constraint. Specifically,
we prove the following lemma:
Lemma 4.14. We can in polynomial-time find a clustering S∗ with k centers of cost at most
                           (1 + 2ε)        ′    ′            ′
                                                               , µ′D ) + O(εopt) .
                                                                      
                                    cost( MO , µO ) + cost( MD
                              2


    Recall that at this point the algorithm calculated the local search solution S, sampled W, guessed
B , Q, and Ue with the assignment µe of the clustering SQ∪Ue . We assume that all these choices were
successful guesses, i.e., that (W, B , Q, Ue, µ
                                              e) is successful. Further recall the notation that we partition
the clients into the clusters {Cc }c∈S e where Cc = { p ∈ D : µ    e( p) = c}.
                                       Q∪U
    Our goal is to give a polynomial-time algorithm that finds approximations of the sets OPT      ˆ and R
that only have slightly worse cost. We start by defining the feasible set of candidates for OPT      ˆ as a
                                   ˆ
partition matroid. Recall that OPT contains one center from each ball in B .

Definition of partition matroid MB . We define the partition matroid that captures the constraint
that we wish to open a center in each ball in B . Let FB be the (multi) subset of facility/center locations
containing B ∩ F for each ball B ∈ B . If a center c is in multiple balls in B , then FB contains one
distinct copy of c for each ball. We let FB ( B) ⊆ FB denote the facilities associated with B ∈ B . These
sets satisfy the following two properties:
   • The sets FB ( B) partition FB .
   • For B ∈ B , FB ( B) contains (a copy of) every facility in F ∩ B.
The first property holds since we took a unique copy of each center for each ball, and they are thus
disjoint: FB ( B) ∩ FB ( B′ ) = ∅ for distinct B, B′ ∈ B . Indeed, while it is not better for the cost to open
multiple copies of a center, we make the copies to ensure the above two properties. This allows us to
define the partition matroid MB = ( FB , I) where

                            I = { X ⊆ FB : | X ∩ FB ( B)| ≤ 1 for every B ∈ B} .
                                                                   ˆ we have OPT
Moreover, since there is exactly one ball in B for each center in OPT,         ˆ ∈ I (where we
                                                               ∗    ˆ
slightly abuse notation as we should take the copy of center c ∈ OPT that belongs to its associated
ball).

Core, concentrated and hit clusters. Our goal is now to define a submodular function f so that we
obtain a good approximation to OPT  ˆ by maximizing f over the matroid constraint MB . In particular,
the domain of f is every subset of FB . However, we need some additional steps before defining f . In
particular, we introduce the concept of the core of a cluster Cc and the notions of concentrated and hit
clusters, which allow us to simplify the structure of centers in R. For a cluster Cc , we define the core
of Cc as
                                                                          
                               core                             cost(S)
                              Cc = p ∈ Cc : d( p, c) ≤ ε ·                   .
                                                               |R| · |Cc |
We further say that cluster Cc is concentrated if

                                            |Cccore | ≥ (1 − ε)|Cc |
and it is hit by a set X of centers if there is a point p ∈ Cccore such that

                                             d( p, X ) < d( p, c) .

For shortness we will sometimes say that a center is concentrated (resp., hit), if the corresponding
cluster is so.


                                                      52
Guessing the centers of R that are not concentrated. We further simplify the task of finding the set
of centers R by guessing the centers of R that are not concentrated. Specifically, partition the set R
into R0 and R1 , where R1 contain those centers of R that are concentrated and R0 contains those that
are not. We can correctly guess R0 in polynomial time since the following simple claim shows that it
is a subset of the centers whose cluster costs at least ε2 · opt/|R|, of which there are only O(|R|/ε2 )
                                                                2
many, and so guessing R0 can thus be done in time 2O(|R|/ε ) .
Claim 8. Suppose that Cc is not concentrated. Then the cost of Cc is at least ε2 · opt/|R|.
Proof. Since Cc is not concentrated we have |Cc − Cccore | ≥ ε|Cc |. Moreover, each point p ∈ Cc − Cccore
                  cost(S)
has d( p, c) > ε |R|·|C | by definition. Hence, as cost(S) ≥ opt,
                      c

                                                                              opt           opt
                    ∑ d( p, c) ≥       ∑ core d( p, c) ≥ |Cc − Cccore | · ε |R| · |Cc | ≥ ε2 |R| .
                   p∈Cc            p∈Cc −Cc




    Now let P be the potential centers that can be in R. Specifically, we let P be the set that contains
a center c in S − Q − Ue if Cc equals the set of clients in SQ (c), where we recall that SQ (c) is the set
of clients that are closest to c in the clustering SQ . Notice that the algorithm has all the information
S, B , Q, Ue and µ
                 e to calculate P . Furthermore, by Lemma 4.13, we have that no client from Ue was
reassigned by µ e to a center in R. So for c ∈ R we have Cc = SQ (c). Hence, R ⊆ P and we can guess
R0 as follows:

  Guessing R0

     1. Let C be the clusters of P whose cost is at least ε2 · opt/|R|.

     2. Output each subset of C .

By the above claim and the definition of P , one of the outputs is R0 . Furthermore, the above guess-
ing procedure outputs a family of polynomial many subsets. Indeed, we have |R| ≤ log(n)/ε3 by
Lemma 4.3. Moreover, the total cost of the clusters in P is O(opt). To see this notice that the clusters
corresponding to P is a subset of the clusters of SQ and the cost of SQ is at most cost(S − S0 ∪ Λ) (since
SQ ⊇ S − S0 ∪ Λ), which has cost at most O(opt) by Lemma 4.9. This implies that |C| = O(log(n)/ε5 ),
                                      O (1)
so the total number of subsets is n1/ε . The algorithm proceeds by trying all possible subsets in the
output, and we analyze the algorithm when it takes the correct guess of R0 .

Definition of the submodular function f . We first define another function g on the same domain
as f , i.e., on all subsets X of FB . We will then define f by f ( X ) = g(∅) − g( X ). Let the closed cost of a
cluster Cc be defined as
                         (
                           ∑ p∈Cc d( p, {c} ∪ X ∪ Λ)                                            if Cc is hit by X,
  closedcostc ( X ) :=                                   ′
                           minc′ ∈X ∪Λ ∑ p∈Cccore d( p, c ) + ∑ p∈Cc −Cccore d( p, {c} ∪ X ∪ Λ) otherwise.

Further, let P1 be the potential centers for R1 : it contains each center c ∈ P − R0 so that c is con-
centrated, i.e., |Cccore | ≥ (1 − ε)|Cc |. We remark that the algorithm can calculate this set P1 as it only
depends on P , the guessed set R0 , the value cost(S), and the clusters Cc defined by µ    e. Moreover, by
definition, we have R1 ⊆ P1 . For a subset X ⊆ FB , we then then define g( X ) to be the minimum
value of

                ∑         ∑ d ( p, {c} ∪ X ∪ Λ) + ∑ ∑ d( p, X ∪ Λ) + ∑ ′ closedcostc (X )

of the solution obtained by removing the centers R0 ∪ R1′ and assigning clients as follows:
   • If p ∈ Cc for a remaining center c ̸∈ R0 ∪ R1′ or p ̸∈ Cccore with c ∈ R1′ , p is assigned to its closest
     center in {c} ∪ X ∪ Λ.


                                                        53
   • If p ∈ Cc for a removed center c ∈ R0 , p is assigned to its closest center in X ∪ Λ.

     Cc , and otherwise all clients in Cccore are assigned to the same center c′ ∈ X ∪ Λ that minimizes
     the cost.
    We remark that this assignment is infeasible in the sense that it may assign clients to removed cen-
ters in R1′ . Nevertheless, we relate the values of g(∅) and g(OPT

of g allows us to prove that f (defined by f ( X ) = g(∅) − g( X )) is a monotone submodular function.
Lemma 4.15. We have that g and f satisfy the following properties:
   1. We can evaluate g( X ) in polynomial time for every X ⊆ FB , and we can thus evaluate f ( X ) in polyno-
      mial time.

    We give the proof of the lemma in the next subsection. We explain here how it implies Lemma 4.14.
Proof of Lemma 4.14. By the first property of Lemma 4.15, we can evaluate f in polynomial time. More-
over, it is easy to see that we can answer independence queries in polynomial time for any partition
matroid, particularly for MB . We can thus apply Theorem 4.1 on f and MB with ζ = (1 − 1/e − 1/2)
to find a solution X ∈ I such that5

                                              O (1)
the number of guesses of R0 is at most n1/ε , as argued after the description of that procedure. So
we can, in polynomial time, try all possibilities and, among all solutions found (one for each guess
of R0 ), return one that minimizes the cost and, in particular, has cost at most that of S∗ (which was
analyzed assuming the guess of R0 was correct). We thus have a polynomial time algorithm that
returns a solution that satisfies the guarantee of the lemma.

     center by c; otherwise, replace the dummy center with an arbitrary center in the ball.

4.7     Putting Everything Together – Proof of Theorem 1.3
We now turn to proving Theorem 1.3. We first describe the algorithm we analyse.

  A (2+ε)-Approximation for k-Median on an ε/ log n- Stable Instance k, D, F, d.

      1: S ← localSearch ()                                                                                              ▷ Section 4.1
      2: W ← D-Sample (S, s∗ )                                                                                           ▷ Section 4.2
      3: Lball ← ballGuess (S, W )                                                                                       ▷ Section 4.3
      4: for all B ∈ Lball do
      5:     Compute the dummy centers Λ according to B
      6:     Lexp ← epxRem(S, Λ)                                                                                         ▷ Section 4.4



                                                                        59
    7:     for all Q ∈ Lexp and all valid values of |U |, |R| and |X | do
    8:          Lcheap ← ∅
     9:         cheapRem(∅, ∅, ∅, ∅, ∅)                                                    ▷ Section 4.5
    10:         for all U ∈ Lcheap with the associated µ
                        e                              e do
    11:              Approximately solve the submodular maximization problem subject to a partition
        matroid induced by (S, W, B , Q, Ue, µ
                                             e) and turn the obtained solution into a k-median solution
        SS,W,B ,Q,Ue,eµ                                                                    ▷ Section 4.6
    12: return the cheapest solution of type SS,W,B ,Q,Ue,e
                                                          µ

Proof of Theorem 1.3. Our approach consists of running the above algorithm log n times and taking the
minimum cost solution output. In the remaining, we argue that the above algorithm yields a 2 + O(ε)-
approximation with probability at least (1 − ε)(1 − 1/n). The claim then follows as the probability
that all log n executions of the above algorithm would fail is at most (1 − (1 − ε)(1 − 1/n))log n < 1/n,
and so with high probability we output a 2 + O(ε)-approximation.
    We first discuss the algorithm’s running time. The local search algorithm runs in polynomial time.
The submodular optimization step also runs in polynomial time by Lemma 4.14. The output of D-
Sample() (which is clearly a polynomial time procedure) is a subset of size s∗ . By Lemma 4.11, we
                                                              O (1)
construct Lball in polynomial time and |Lball | ≤ n1/ε . Similarly, by Lemma 4.11, we construct
                                                 O ( 1 )
Lexp in polynomial time and |Lexp | ≤ n1/ε . Finally, by Lemma 4.13, we construct in polynomial-
           O (1)                                                      O (1)
time n1/ε                            e. It follows that we solve n1/ε
                 candidate pairs Ue, µ                                      submodular function optimization
problems, each in polynomial time, and thus the total running time is polynomial.
    We turn to proving the approximation guarantee. We aim to show that there exists a (W, B , Q, Ue, µ    e)
that is successful with probability at least (1 − ε)(1 − 1/n). Assuming this, the (2 + O(ε))-approximation
follows by combining Claim 7 and Lemma 4.14.
    The local search step provides a 5 approximation S that satisfies Lemma 4.4. Our algorithm next
applies D-Sample() and by Lemma 4.5 finds a successful W with probability at least 1 − ε. Condition
on having a successful W, we then have that Lemma 4.6 holds, and so does Lemma 4.7 by combining
Lemma 4.4 and Lemma 4.6.
    Next, Lemma 4.8 implies that one set of balls B is a valid set of balls. This set of balls induces a set
of dummy centers Λ. From there, by Lemma 4.11 we have that with probability at least 1 − 1/n, the
expRem() procedure produces a set of center Q such that
    1. Q ⊆ S0 , and
    2. The total cost in solution S − Q ∪ Λ of the clusters in S0 − Q is at most ε · opt.
and so we have obtained a successful (W, B , Q) and Lemma 4.12 applies. Condition on the event that
we obtain a successful (W, B , Q), then, applying Lemma 4.13 we have that the cheapRem() procedure
outputs a pair (Ue, µ e) satisfying the properties of Lemma 4.13. We thus have a (W, B , Q, Ue, µ   e) such
that W is successful, the set of balls B from Section 4.3 is valid, Q selected in Section 4.4 satisfies the
properties of Lemma 4.11, and Ue, µ   e selected in this section satisfies the properties of Lemma 4.13 and
so (W, B , Q, U , µ
              e   e) is successful. Finally, note that the only probabilistic steps were the sampling of W
and of Q, and both steps are successful with probability at least (1 − ε) · (1 − 1/n), as required.


A        Analysis of Robust L OG A DAPTIVE
In this section, we prove Theorem 3.10, showing that a solution H of η-valid sequences yields an
almost 2-LMP approximation. The proof is almost identical to that for L OG A DAPTIVE presented in
Section 3.3. There are only two minor changes:
    1. Definition 3 of η-openability allows a facility to be paid up to an nη-additive difference. This
       difference only appears in Lemma A.1 below, which is an analog of Lemma 3.7.

                                                                         ∗ ( fˆ − nη ).
         Lemma A.1. We have ∑ j∈ D α∗j ≥ (1 − δ) ∑ j∈ D d( j, S∗ ) + ∑i∈Sreg



                                                        60
   2. Definiton 4 of a η-valid sequence allows ht is η-openable with respect to a super set S′ ⊇ S ∪
      {h1 , . . . , ht−1 }. This difference only appears in (the last part of) the proof of Lemma A.2, an
      analog of Lemma 3.8.
      Lemma A.2. For a facility i, ∑ j∈ D [α∗j − 2d(i, j)]+ ≤ fˆ.

These two lemmas immediately imply Theorem 3.10. The other supporting claims and lemmas
(Claim 10 and Lemma A.3) remain identical.
    In order to use mostly the same analysis, one syntactic difference between the setting of Sec-
tion 3.3 and the current setting that we have to reconcile is that the former analyzes the outcome
of L OG A DAPTIVE while the latter analyzes a solution H = { H1 , . . . , HL } of η-valid sequences. To
use the same terminology from Section 3.3, let us consider the execution of the solution H where for
p = 1, . . . , L, we run the p-th phase according to the sequence H p ; in Stage 1, each facility in the
sequence becomes open one by one with the corresponding (τj ) j values, and in Stage 2 at the end of
the phase, θ ← θ (1 + ε2 ).
    Then an atomic step can still be defined in the same way as in L OG A DAPTIVE; it corresponds
to opening a facility in Stage 1 (and updating α, S, A accordingly) or increasing α values in Stage 2
simultaneously (and updating S accordingly). By “at any point of the algorithm”, we mean any point
in the algorithm’s execution that is not in the middle of an atomic step. The set of open facilities S
can be partitioned to be the set of the open free facilities Sfree and the set of the open regular facilities
Sreg .
    Throughout the analysis, we let α∗j be the final α-value of a client j ∈ D, and we let S∗ be the
                                      ∗
set of opened facilities. (Also let Sfree       ∗ be the set of finally open free and regular facilities
                                          and Sreg
respectively.) We start by analyzing the approximation guarantee of the algorithm with respect to α∗ ,
and we then prove that α∗ /2 is a feasible solution to the dual.

A.1    Approximation Guarantee.
We prove Lemma A.1 restated below. The only difference from Lemma 3.7 is that opening a facility
only requires fˆ − nη instead of fˆ.
                                                                ∗ ( fˆ − nη ).
Lemma A.1. We have ∑ j∈ D α∗j ≥ (1 − δ) ∑ j∈ D d( j, S∗ ) + ∑i∈Sreg
Proof of Lemma A.1. The proof is by induction on the algorithm that at any point of the algorithm

                                  ∑ α j ≥ ∑ (1 − δ)d( j, S) + ∑ ( fˆ − nη ) .                                 (32)
                                  j∈ I           j∈ I               i ∈Sreg

The equality is initially true since A = D (thus I = ∅) and S = ∅. We now analyze each of the two
stages separately.

Stage 1. Consider what happens when we open a facility h, i.e., add it to S.
    If h is a free facility, the α values and Sreg do not change, and the only change is some client j with
α j ≥ (1 − δ)d( j, S) becoming inactive. The amount of the increase of the left-hand side of (32) is at
least that of the right-hand side.
    If h is a regular facility i, let (α, S, A, θ ) be the state right before opening i, A′ be the clients that are
removed from A by the opening, which means A′ = { j ∈ A : τj ≥ (1 − δ)d(i, j)}. (Recall that the
opening lets α j ← τj for j ∈ A.) Further, let X := { j ∈ I : d( j, i ) < d( j, S)} be the subset of clients of I
that make positive bids to i. The change of cost of the right-hand side of (32) is at most

                        ( fˆ − nη ) + ∑ (1 − δ)d(i, j) + ∑ (1 − δ)(d(i, j) − d( j, S)) .
                                         j∈ A′               j∈ X

Since i is paid up to η (the third bullet of Definition 3), we also have

                     fˆ − nη ≤ ∑ (τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)) + ∑ (1 − δ)(d( j, S) − d(i, j)) .
                                j∈ A′                           j∈ X

We thus get that the change of cost of the right-hand-side is at most ∑ j∈ A′ τj , which is the change of
the left-hand-side.


                                                        61
Stage 2. No facility is open at this stage, and every client j that becomes inactive at this stage has
α j = (1 − δ)d( j, S), which implies that the left-hand-side and right-hand-side both increase with the
same amount when a client j becomes inactive.

A.2    Dual Feasibility
We prove Lemma A.2 for dual feasibility. The following claim is identical to Claim 1 without any
modification.
Claim 10 (No Over-Bidding). At any point of the algorithm, for every facility i,

                            ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ.                      (33)
                           j∈ A                  j∈ I



Proof. In the beginning, it is true because every client is active and α j = 1 ≤ d(i, j) for every j ∈ D.
Let us prove that each step of the algorithm maintains this invariant. We first consider the steps that
happen during the first stage and then those during the second stage.

Stage 1. Suppose that facility h′ is open with the bids (τj ) j∈ A . It does (1) potentially increase the α-
values of clients in B(h′ , εθ ) ∩ A, and (2) make clients in A ∩ B(h′ , θ/(1 − δ)) inactive, which includes
B(h′ , εθ ) ∩ A considered in (1).
      Consider any facility i and see how (33) is impacted by this change. First, observe that a client j
that was inactive before the opening of h′ does not increase its contribution to (33). This is because
d( j, S) monotonically decreases in S. We now turn our attention to the more interesting case. For
a client j becoming inactive from active, the contribution to (33) changes from [α j − d(i, j)]+ (where
α-values are measured right before the opening) to [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i )]+ (where S includes h′ ).
      If α j does not change by the opening of h′ , the fact that j becomes inactive means (1 − δ)d( j, S) ≤
α j , so j’s contribution to (33) cannot increase. If α j does strictly increase by the opening of h′ , it means
that d( j, S) ≤ d( j, h′ ) ≤ εθ after the opening, so the contribution to (33) decreases by at least θ/2 or
becomes 0.
      We have thus proved that no client increases their contribution to (33) during the steps of the first
stage, and so these steps maintain the inequality.

Stage 2. Assume towards contradiction that at the end of the phase, if we increase α j of every j ∈ A
to min((1 + ε2 )θ, (1 − δ)d( j, S)), the claim is violated for a non-empty subset of facilities. Since any
free facility is only farther away in the metric (depending on u(·)) we thus must have that the claim is
violated by a set of regular facilities. Let F ′ be that set. We select a “minimal” such counter example
in the following way: let τ ′ ≤ (1 + ε2 )θ be the smallest value so that α′j := min(τ ′ , (1 − δ)d( j, S))
satisfies
                            ∑ [α′j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ = fˆ,
                           j∈ A                  j∈ I

for some i ∈ F ′ . We remark that τ ′ ≥ θ since (33) is satisfied for all facilities after the first stage by the
previous arguments. Furthermore, i ̸∈ S is not yet open since if it was open then there would be no
client j ∈ A for which α′j − d(i, j) is strictly positive. Hence the increase in α-values cannot cause i to
violate (33) if it were already opened, which would contradict i ∈ F ′ .
    We now show that this i with τj = α′j for j ∈ A ∩ B(i, εθ ) and τj = α j for j ∈ A \ B(i, εθ ) satisfies
the conditions of Definition 3 for i with respect to S. In other words, i is an η-openable facility with
respect to S (that was not yet opened), which contradicts the maximality in Definition 4 for a η-valid
sequence.
    We verify the conditions of Definition 3 one-by-one. The first two bullets are satisfied by the




                                                        62
definition of the bids (τj ) j∈ A . For the third bullet,

         ∑ [τj − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ [d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+
       j∈ A                                                j∈ I

  =           ∑         [α′j − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ +                 ∑        [α j − (1 − δ)d(i, j)]+ + (1 − δ) ∑ [d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+
       j∈ A∩ B(i,εθ )                                     j∈ A\ B(i,εθ )                                         j∈ I

  ≥           ∑         [α′j − d(i, j)]+ +           ∑           [α′j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+ .
       j∈ A∩ B(i,εθ )                           j∈ A\ B(i,εθ )                          j∈ I


For the second summation we used that α j − (1 − δ)d(i, j) ≥ (1 + ε2 )α j − d(i, j) ≥ α′j − d(i, j) when
d(i, j) ≥ εθ (recall that α j = θ for all j ∈ A).
    It remains to verify the fourth bullet of Definition 3. Suppose toward contradiction that there is a
regular facility i0 and k ∈ A,

                                 ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [τk − d(i0 , k) − 2d(i0 , j)]+ > fˆ.
                                 j∈ A                               j∈ I

We would like to show

                                          ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i0 , j)]+
                                         j∈ A                              j∈ I

                                 ≥        ∑ [τj − d(i0 , j)] + ∑ [τk − d(i0 , k) − 2d(i0 , j)]+
                                                                    +
                                         j∈ A                              j∈ I

which concludes the proof because then i0 is violated with a smaller value of τ ′ which contradicts the
minimal selection of τ ′ . To prove the above inequality, note that k ∈ A implies τk ≤ (1 − δ)d(k, S)
and for every j ∈ I,
                           (1 − δ)d(k, S) ≤ (1 − δ)(d(k, i0 ) + d(i0 , j) + d( j, S)),
and so
                                     τk − d(i0 , k ) − 2d(i0 , j) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i0 , j) .


   As usual, fix an arbitrary regular facility i ∈ F. The setup in this paragraph is also identical to
Section 3.3. Let D ∗ = { j ∈ D : α∗j > 2d(i, j)} be those clients that contribute to the left-hand-side.
Further, order the clients in D ∗ according to α∗ values by the time they are removed from A (in the
algorithm) and break ties according to α∗ values (in increasing order). We will use j, k to denote clients
in D ∗ , and let us slightly abuse notation and use j ≤ k with respect to this ordering. In other words,
we have j ≤ k if j was removed from A in an atomic step before k was removed, j and k was removed
during the same atomic step and α∗j ≤ α∗k . We also use the terminology that a facility i freezes i0 if i
becomes open when i0 is not, and d(i, i0 ) ≤ (1 + 3ε)θ/2. (Of course, i freezes itself when it is open.)
   The following lemma and the proof are also identical to Lemma 3.9.

Lemma A.3. For any k ∈ D ∗ that becomes inactive strictly before i becomes frozen,

                                ∑
                                ∗
                                         [α∗k − d(i, j)]+ +                ∑
                                                                           ∗
                                                                                  [α∗k − 2d(i, j) − d(i, k)]+ ≤ fˆ.               (34)
                             j∈ D :j≥k                              j∈ D :j<k



Proof. Let us do the following case analysis depending on the stage k becomes inactive.

Stage 1. k becomes inactive because of the opening of some h0 . By the statement of the lemma, it
suffices to handle the case that h0 does not freeze i. So d(i, h0 ) > (1 + 3ε)θ/2. The opening of h0 might
have strictly increased the α-value of the clients in B(h0 , εθ ), but no such client j will be in D ∗ , since
they immediately become inactive while

                    d(i, j) ≥ d(i, h0 ) − d(h0 , j) > ((1 + 3ε)/2 − ε)θ ≥ (1 + ε2 )θ/2 ≥ α∗j /2.


                                                                           63
Applying this argument to every facility open in this phase (which did not freeze i), we can conclude
that, right before h0 is open, every j ∈ D ∗ has α j ≤ θ and all active ones have α j = θ. (So, α∗k = θ as
well since the opening of h0 does not strictly increase αk .) Claim 10, applied right before h0 is open,
ensures that
                           ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i, j)]+ ≤ fˆ.
                           j∈ A                  j∈ I

This satisfies (34) as (1) every j ∈ D ∗ with j ≥ k is in j ∈ A at that point, and (2) for every j < k with
j ∈ I,
                          α∗k < (1 − δ)d(k, S) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, S) + d( j, i ) + d(i, k )
implies
                              α∗k − 2d(i, j) − d(i, k ) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i, j).
(Note that this S does not contain h0 and k was not connected yet.)

Stage 2. Suppose that k becomes inactive by the increase of the α-values at the end of a phase. Then
Claim 10 ensures that, at the end of this phase (after k becomes inactive),

                           ∑ [α j − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ.                    (35)
                           j∈ A                  j∈ I

    Let us see that the left-hand-side of (35) is at least that of (34) by comparing the contribution of
j ∈ D ∗ to both. If j ∈ A, its contribution to the former is definitely at least that to the latter, since any
active j has α j = (1 + ε2 )θ ≥ α∗k .
    For j ∈ I, we must have

                             α∗k ≤ (1 − δ)d(k, S) ≤ (1 − δ)(d(k, j) + d( j, S)) .

(since αk is never increased above (1 − δ)d(k, S)). and so by rearranging and using the triangle in-
equality (d(k, j) ≤ d(k, i ) + d(i, j)) we get

                              α∗k − 2d(i, j) − d(i, k ) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, S) − d(i, j)

which satisfies our goal when j < k. Finally, when j ∈ I but j ≥ k, it means that j was removed at
the same time as k at the end of the phase. In that case (1 − δ)d( j, S) = α∗j and, as we break ties with
α∗ -values, α∗j ≥ α∗k , which again satisfies our goal.

   Equipped with the above lemma, we are ready to complete the proof of dual feasibility.
Proof of Lemma A.2. Consider the case that i is frozen by h0 with (τj ) j∈ A . The following claim also
remains unchanged from Section 3.3.
Claim. We have α∗j = τj for every j ∈ D ∗ ∩ A.

Proof of Claim. Consider j ∈ A. First, note that if τj ≥ (1 − δ)d( j, h0 ) then j is removed from A when
h0 is opened and so α∗j = τj .
    In the other case, when α j ≤ τj < (1 − δ)d( j, h0 ) we show that j ̸∈ D ∗ . Consider the (future) time
right after j is removed from the active clients. The α-value of j then (which is equal to the final α∗j )
cannot be strictly greater than (1 − δ)d( j, h0 ); whether it is increased in Stage 1 (as α ← τ) or Stage
2, since h0 ∈ S (where S is the set of open facilities right before j becomes inactive), the algorithm
ensures that it cannot be strictly more than (1 − δ)d( j, S) ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, h0 ). By the triangle inequality
                                                                                       
                                                                d ( h0 , i )     θ
                      d( j, i ) ≥ d( j, h0 ) − d(h0 , i ) = 1 −                           d( j, h0 ) ,
                                                                     θ       d( j, h0 )
where θ is the value when h0 was open. As h0 freezes i, we have d(h0 , i ) ≤ (1 + 3ε)θ/2 and, by the
assumption of the case we have θ = α j ≤ (1 − δ)d( j, h0 ). Plugging in those bounds to the above
inequality yields,
                                                            
                                             (1 + 3ε)
                             d( j, i ) ≥ 1 −          (1 − δ) d( j, h0 ) .
                                                2


                                                        64
We rewrite the above inequality (multiplying both sides by two and using that δ = 3ε) to obtain
                                                                          
                    2d( j, i ) ≥ (2 − (1 + 3ε)(1 − δ)) d( j, h0 ) = 1 + 9ε2 d( j, h0 ) .

In other words we have α∗j ≤ d( j, h0 ) < 2d( j, i ), so j cannot be in D ∗ .

    The following remaining part of the proof needs some changes from Section 3.3, since when h0 is
η-openable in the η-openable sequence, it might be with respect to a superset S′ ⊇ S. (Here S denotes
the set of all opened facilities right before h0 .) Let A′ the subset of A that is still active with respected
to the superset S′ for opening h0 and let I ′ = A \ A′ . As the α-values of clients in A ∩ D ∗ remain
unchanged after the opening of h0 , the fourth bullet of Definition 3 ensures that for any k ∈ A′ ∩ D ∗ ,

                        ∑
                        ′ ∗
                            [α∗j − d(i, j)]+ +                 ∑         [α∗k − 2d(i, j) − d(k, i )]+ ≤ fˆ.   (36)
                      j∈ A ∩ D                     j∈( I ∪ I ′ )∩ D ∗

   Moreover, for every client k ∈ D ∗ ∩ I that became inactive strictly before h0 was opened, Lemma A.3
says

                          ∑
                          ∗
                                   [α∗k − d(i, j)]+ +          ∑
                                                               ∗
                                                                        [α∗k − 2d(i, j) − d(i, k)]+ ≤ fˆ.     (37)
                       j∈ D :j≥k                        j∈ D :j<k

    Finally for a client in k ∈ D ∗ ∩ I ′ , note that α∗k = αk = θ, because k was not active with respect to
 ′
S . Therefore, by Claim 10 applied right before h0 was opened

                             ∑ [α∗k − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [(1 − δ)d( j, S) − d( j, i)]+ ≤ fˆ.
                            j∈ A                        j∈ I

which by the same argument as in the proof of Lemma 3.8,

                             ∑ [α∗k − d(i, j)]+ + ∑ [α∗k − 2d( j, i) − d(i, k)]+ ≤ fˆ.                        (38)
                             j∈ A                       j∈ I

      Let us consider the summation of (37) for every k ∈ I ∩ D ∗ , (38) for every k ∈ I ′ ∩ D ∗ , and (36)
for every k ∈ A′ ∩ D ∗ , and consider how many times each term appears. Let a′ = | A′ ∩ D ∗ |, i′ =
| I ′ ∩ D ∗ |, d = | D ∗ |.

   • −d(i, j′ ) when j′ ∈ I ∩ D ∗ : Say j′ is the ℓth client in D ∗ for some ℓ ≤ d − i′ − a′ . Then −d(i, j′ )
     appears d + (d − ℓ) times in (36), (37), and (38) as −d(i, j), and (ℓ − 1) times as −d(i, k ) in (37)
     when k = j′ , so the total is 2d − 1.

   • −d(i, j′ ) when j′ ∈ I ′ ∩ D ∗ : It appears d + a′ times in (36), (37), and (38) as −d(i, j), and (d − i′ −
     a′ ) times as −d(i, k ) in (38) when k = j′ , so the total is 2d − i′ .
   • −d(i, j′ ) when j′ ∈ A′ ∩ D ∗ : It appears d times in (36), (37), and (38) as −d(i, j), and (d − a′ )
     times as −d(i, k ) in (36) when k = j′ , so the total is 2d − a′ .

   • α∗j′ for j′ ∈ I ∩ D ∗ : d times in (37) as α∗k when k = j′ .

   • α∗j′ for j′ ∈ I ′ ∩ D ∗ : d times in (38) as α∗k when k = j′ .

   • α∗j′ for j′ ∈ A′ ∩ D ∗ : (d − a′ ) times in (36) as α∗k when k = j′ and once as α∗j in (36) for each k ∈ A′ ,
      so it is still d times.
                                  
Therefore, ∑ j∈ D∗ α∗j − 2d(i, j) ≤ fˆ, which proves the lemma in the case the opening of a facility h0
freezes i. To complete the proof, we note that the remaining case, when no facility freezes i, corre-
sponds to the easier situation in the above analysis
                                                           whenD ∗ ∩ A = ∅, i.e., it corresponds to summing
up (37) over all clients in D ∗ , and so ∑ j∈ D∗ α∗ − 2d(i, j) ≤ fˆ also in that case.
